Simeone 4 of 283 Section 1: Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Section 1: Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Comprehensive Education Planning (CEP) is a structured, data-driven process that guides schools in assessing their needs, identifying areas for growth, setting strategic goals and implementing targeted actions to improve student outcomes. Collaboratively developed by the School Leadership Team (SLT) in accordance with the requirements of Chancellor’s Regulation A-655, the CEP meets New York State Education Department (NYSED) requirements under the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA) and aligns with NYC Public Schools’ (NYCPS) system-wide priorities. By engaging all stakeholders—school leaders, teachers, families, students and community members—schools can create a data-informed, equity- focused roadmap for student success and continuous improvement. Schools should focus their CEP on the five system-wide priorities below, connecting their planning to the data discussions and conversations with stakeholders held as part of the school’s needs assessment: Priority 1: All students learn to read well Priority 2: All students are physically and emotionally safe Priority 3: All students have a high-quality academic experience Priority 4: All students graduate college and career ready and have a strong plan and pathway to economic security Priority 5: All districts and schools are more inclusive and responsive for parents and families, including having more families choose NYC Public Schools The Education Planning Process The CEP follows an equity-driven, continuous improvement cycle, rooted in the system-wide priorities. This begins with a comprehensive needs assessment of the school’s most current data to identify priority needs and root causes. Next, the SLT establishes specific, measurable, attainable, relevant, and timely— SMART goal(s) aligned to each priority and determines targets to measure progress toward meeting the annual goals. The SLT and instructional leaders define key evidence-based key strategies, professional learning activities and parent, family and community empowerment efforts. They monitor the implementation of those strategies and activities and track progress through measurable targets. Then, the SLT aligns the school-based budget with human and instructional resources to support action plans. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 5 of 283 As schools set goals, develop key strategies, and plan action steps, they provide opportunities for all children to meet NYSED’s proficient and advanced levels of student academic achievement. These include evidence based instructional strategies that address the educational needs of historically underserved populations and tackle structural inequities. Schools indicate how the plan addresses the needs of Multilingual/English Language Learners (ML/ELL) through development of the school’s Language Allocation Policy (LAP) & Title III Application (if applicable) and the Language Translation and Interpretation (LTI) Plan, that are required as part of the CEP. Schools also address the needs of Students with Disabilities (SWD) through the High Quality Inclusive & Accessible Learning for Students with Disabilities section of the plan. SLTs also develop a plan to improve attendance and reduce chronic absenteeism. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 6 of 283 Guidance on Conducting a Comprehensive Needs Assessment Directions: Conduct a comprehensive needs assessment for each Priority. The information below provides guidance for reviewing and analyzing student and school performance trends to uncover root causes for low student achievement. 1. Review Multiple Data Sources: Use the most current and available information including, but not limited to, the following sources of formative and summative data, as applicable: Qualitative and quantitative data, including enrollment and class size trends 2024-25 ESSA Accountability Data Instructional Leadership Framework (ILF), Supportive Environment Framework (SEF) Inventories, and NYC School Survey results Quality Review Reports, NYSED and NYCPS CSI or A/TSI Support Visits, Receivership Quarterly Reports, and Diagnostic Tool for School and District Effectiveness Framework (DTSDE) NYC School Quality Guide, Insight, New Visions, NWEA MAP and/or other formative assessment data, IEP Review Survey Data, Progress on IEP Goals Data, Program (SESIS-STARS Linkage) Reports, Mandated (Related) Services Reports, and NYCPS Tools for Understanding Your Data for student subgroup information 2. Analyze Data Trend(s) to Identify Areas of Strength and Need: Identify data trends by grade, cohort, and content area(s) to surface gaps and inequities. Compare data sets to identify instructional and school practices that are impacting student performance. Guiding questions to consider: What does the data reveal about improving and declining student performance trends within grades, cohorts, and student subgroups? What inequities are evident between highest and lowest performing student subgroups? Which subgroup(s) scored at Level 1 based on NYSED ESSA accountability indicators? What does the data suggest regarding the effectiveness (or ineffectiveness) of current school programs, practices, and/or strategies/initiatives? What areas may need to be strengthened? Are there any differences in student performance between program delivery models for ML/ELL and SWD? (e.g., for special education: special class, ICT, SETSS, related services only; for ML/ELL: Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) vs. Dual Language, etc.) How does delivery of instruction factor into data trends? What are the implications for teacher quality and professional learning? What inequities in special education referral and school disciplinary practices (e.g. suspensions, removals) are evident between student subgroups with IEPs? 3. Identify Root Cause(s): After analyzing the data, engage in a process for uncovering root causes or contributing factors for low student and school performance. Develop theories or hypotheses to explain DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 7 of 283 why these needs exist and why your school is getting its current outcomes. Root causes could be related to gaps and inequities in areas such as curriculum & instruction, school systems & structures, student support services, school climate & safety, and family & community engagement. Focus on the causes, rather than the symptoms. Use the 5 Whys Method or other methods for identifying root causes. Consider the questions below: What theories or hypotheses explain the current student and school outcomes? Are there root causes specific to certain subgroups, grades, cohorts, or content area(s)? Additional resources and supports are available below. Use this CEP Needs Assessment Worksheet to help your SLT and stakeholders identify appropriate data sources, data trends, root causes and priority needs. For guidance and best practices on engaging stakeholders in a comprehensive needs assessment and root cause analysis, see this deck. A current list of all CEP/Title I resources can be found on the iPlan Portal Public Resources Page. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 11 of 283 Section 3: CEP SLT/Stakeholder Signature Page Section 3: CEP SLT/Stakeholder Signature Page School Leadership Team (SLT) Signature Page All School Leadership Team (SLT) members are expected to sign this page to confirm their participation in the development of this Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP) and that they have been consulted regarding the alignment of the school-based budget to support this school’s educational program, which includes annual goals and action plans, Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS), the Parent and Family Engagement Policy (PFEP) and the School-Parent Compact (SPC). The SLT must include an equal number of parents and staff and have a minimum of 10 and a maximum of 17 members, in accordance with Chancellor’s Regulation A-655. The SLT also serves as the Core Community Engagement Team (CET) for Receivership Schools. Additional information and resources are available on the School Leadership Team Resources page. Under the Federal Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), NYS Education Law and Commissioner’s Regulations, stakeholder participation in the development of the CEP must include multiple constituencies who represent the school community. Note: Stakeholders participating in the development of the CEP who are not listed on the SLT page sign the section below entitled Additional Stakeholder Participation on CEP Development. Simeone 15 of 283 School Summary Directions: Complete this CEP Summary to serve as the “at-a-glance” narrative that provides contextual information about your school’s unique characteristics. This summary and the entire Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP) should be shared with your school community. Please note that this summary and the CEP will be made available to the public through the iPlan Portal, an online space where school stakeholders can go to collaborate in the development of the school’s education plans. Provide your school’s mission statement: Our Mission at P.S. 7 Queens is to create an environment that addresses the needs of our entire school community. All students, faculty, parents and members of the community will work collaboratively to achieve optimum learning for everyone. We will accomplish this by customizing instruction for our differentiated learners through standards- based practices embedded in the Next Generations Standards Curriculum that will ensure success for all. Develop a narrative summary that includes contextual information about your school’s community and its unique/important characteristics and partnerships: Include demographic information about your school’s student population. Our vision at P.S. 7 Queens is to create a school community where teachers are instructional leaders, who work collaboratively in a risk-free learning environment that is safe, stimulating, and challenging. Our learning community will enable our students to take their places as active and responsible members of our society and the world, embracing the cultural privileges of American citizenship while retaining pride in their ethnic heritage. Our mission is to organize and utilize all resources to address the needs of our entire school community. All students, faculty, parents, and members of the community will consistently achieve optimum learning for everyone through reflective dialogue and shared best practices. We commit to this endeavor by focusing on each student and customizing differentiated instruction that is based on standards and integrated in the arts and technology to ensure academic excellence for all of our students. Briefly summarize the key initiatives your school will implement this school year. Key initiatives our school had implemented this school year is the implementation of the HMH Into Reading literacy curriculum and strengthening practices in the K-2 Fundations program. HMH Into Reading program reflects the most conclusive reading and writing research and evidence-based effective classroom practices. Authored by leaders in the field, Into Reading is built upon the Science of Reading, a foundation of research proven to be effective in raising student achievement. Fundations systematically and comprehensively instructs students in phonemic awareness and word study (both phonetic and high frequency/sight words) and contributes greatly to fluency, vocabulary development, and the applications of strategies for understanding text. Simeone 27 of 283 Priority 1 Action Plan Directions: Identify the key strategy and progressive action steps your school will take to address the specific priority needs and root causes identified for this goal(s). More than one strategy can be indicated if multiple and/or differentiated needs for this priority have been identified. Key strategies should be key levers for success and may represent something that is new to the school. Action steps to operationalize key strategies should focus on new activities or initiatives and/or modifications to current initiatives (e.g., expanded to reach a wider audience or refined for greater impact on students). Note: Routine activities your school does every year should not be included. Key Strategy 1 (Required): At PS7Q, we are committed to strengthening literacy in our lower grades by focusing on phonics skills through the implementation of the Fundations and Heggerty programs. Recognizing that a strong foundation in phonics is essential for early reading success, we will ensure these research-based programs are delivered consistently and effectively across all K-2 classrooms. To achieve this, our teachers will receive comprehensive professional development and ongoing coaching to build their capacity in using Fundations and Heggerty with fidelity. Regular use of Acadience Reading assessments will allow us to monitor student progress closely, identify those who need additional support, and adjust instruction to meet individual needs. In addition to classroom instruction, we will engage families by offering workshops and resources that empower parents to support phonics practice at home, strengthening the connection between school and home learning environments. Teachers will collaborate during grade-level planning sessions to share best practices, analyze student data, and design targeted interventions to address varied learner profiles. We will also ensure that classrooms are equipped with all necessary instructional materials, providing students with the tools they need to develop strong phonics skills. Through these combined efforts, we aim to increase the percentage of kindergarten students performing at or above the Acadience Reading benchmark significantly, closing achievement gaps and fostering equitable literacy outcomes for all students. Root Cause Addressed: The root cause is addressed by implementing a consistent, school-wide approach to phonics instruction through the Fundations and Heggerty programs, which provide explicit, systematic, and evidence-based teaching of foundational reading skills. By offering targeted professional development and ongoing coaching, teachers enhance their instructional practices to ensure fidelity and effectiveness. Regular use of formative assessments like Acadience Reading helps identify students’ needs early, allowing for timely interventions. Additionally, engaging DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 28 of 283 families with workshops and resources supports literacy development beyond the classroom, helping to mitigate disparities caused by varied home literacy environments. Together, these strategies reduce instructional inconsistencies and build strong literacy foundations, addressing the underlying causes of uneven early reading skills. Action Steps: Indicate what your school will do specifically to implement each strategy to achieve this annual goal. Include both student-facing activities and professional learning for staff. Be sure to indicate how action steps will be introduced and rolled out. Action Steps Facilitate PD for teachers, AIS, and ENL staff on the characteristics of dyslexia, using early screening data, and structured literacy interventions. Appoint a literacy point-person to lead implementation of NYC Reads-aligned practices and serve as a liaison with district dyslexia initiatives. Student-Facing Actions: Small-group phonics instruction will be delivered in K–2 classrooms using the Fundations program, tailored based on Acadience Reading data to target students’ specific skill gaps. This targeted instruction will focus on explicit, systematic phonics skills to build foundational reading abilities. Additionally, supplemental phonemic awareness activities using Heggerty will support early literacy development. Professional Learning for Staff: Teachers and instructional aides will participate in ongoing professional development focused on evidence-based phonics instruction using Fundations and Heggerty. Training will include modeling lessons, data analysis to inform instruction, and collaborative planning time. Rollout and Introduction: The action steps will be introduced at the start of the school year through professional development sessions and teacher planning meetings. Small-group instruction schedules will be developed by grade- level teams, with continuous monitoring and adjustments based on Acadience data at BOY, MOY, and EOY checkpoints. Regular coaching and support will be provided to ensure fidelity of implementation. Key Strategy 2 (Optional): NA Root Cause Addressed: NA Action Steps: DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Human Resources PS7Q will leverage the expertise of a full-time Single Point of Contact (SPOC) to coordinate literacy initiatives and serve as a liaison between staff, families, and external partners. This role is essential for ensuring cohesive implementation and communication. Additionally, literacy coaches will provide targeted support through instructional modeling, professional development, and data analysis to guide teaching practices. Grade-level teachers in K–2 are critical human resources, delivering daily phonics instruction and utilizing assessment data to inform instruction. Instructional resources include the Fundations and Heggerty programs, which provide the structured curriculum and materials needed for effective phonics and phonemic awareness instruction. Together, these human and instructional resources form a comprehensive support system aligned with the action plan to strengthen literacy outcomes. Instructional Resources The school will utilize evidence-based phonics programs such as Fundations and Heggerty to provide systematic, explicit phonics instruction. Google Classroom will support instructional delivery, communication, and sharing of resources with students and families. Supplemental materials for phonics and literacy instruction will also be used to reinforce learning. Schedule Adjustments No major schedule changes are anticipated; however, time will be allocated during professional development sessions and grade-level collaboration meetings to focus on literacy coaching, data review, and planning targeted interventions. Other Resources Needed Additional professional development focused on effective phonics instruction and culturally responsive teaching strategies will be sought to enhance teacher capacity. Family engagement materials and translation services will be needed to support communication and workshops for diverse families. Funding may be explored to support these additional training and family outreach efforts. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 42 of 283 Priority 2 Action Plan Directions: Identify the key strategy and progressive action steps your school will take to address the specific priority needs and root causes identified for this goal(s). More than one strategy can be indicated if multiple and/or differentiated needs for this priority have been identified. Key strategies should be key levers for success and may represent something that is new to the school. Action steps to operationalize key strategies should focus on new activities or initiatives and/or modifications to current initiatives (e.g., expanded to reach a wider audience or refined for greater impact on students). Note: Routine activities your school does every year should not be included. Key Strategy 1 (Required): By June 2026, P.S. 007 will improve student physical and mental wellness, increasing the percentage of students who can distinguish between conflict and bullying from 84% to 87.5%, and reducing bullying, intimidation, and bias- based harassment incidents by at least two, as measured by OORS. To achieve this, the school will strengthen Tier 1 social-emotional supports through weekly SEL morning meetings using Second Step and Sanford Harmony. Monthly guidance lessons will focus on conflict resolution and bullying prevention, reinforced during Respect for All Week and other schoolwide initiatives. A Student Equity & Wellness Leadership Group will be formed to promote student voice, lead assemblies, and gather peer feedback through surveys and safe space circles. Staff will receive professional development in trauma- informed practices and restorative approaches to strengthen classroom culture. Family workshops will support mental wellness and bullying awareness at home. Specific supports and outreach strategies will be implemented for Students in Temporary Housing and Foster Care, including prioritized access to counseling services, targeted social-emotional learning groups, and regular check-ins by dedicated staff to ensure their needs are met within the MTSS framework. At-risk students, including those in temporary housing or foster care, will be monitored through an MTSS system, with access to counseling and small group interventions as needed. Progress will be monitored using OORS reports, student surveys, and behavior data, ensuring all students feel safe, supported, and ready to learn. Root Cause Addressed: The root cause—behavioral challenges stemming from unmet emotional needs, unstable home environments, and inconsistent behavioral supports—has been identified through an increase in behavioral incidents reported in the OORS data, dips in student attendance, and survey results indicating students’ feelings of emotional distress and disengagement. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 43 of 283 P.S. 007 will address these challenges through a comprehensive, schoolwide approach that prioritizes early intervention, consistent SEL instruction, and strong student-staff relationships. To expand Tier 1 SEL instruction, the school will implement structured weekly morning meetings focused on emotional regulation and relationship-building. To ensure timely support for students struggling with behavior or emotional regulation, a Multi-Tiered System of Supports (MTSS) will be used, leveraging behavioral data, attendance patterns, and counseling referrals to guide targeted interventions. All staff will engage in professional development on restorative practices, trauma-informed instruction, and de- escalation strategies to promote consistent responses to behavioral challenges across classrooms. At-risk students will receive individual or small group counseling, check-in/check-out supports, and structured mentoring to meet their emotional and social needs. The school will also strengthen partnerships with families through parent workshops and regular communication, equipping caregivers to recognize signs of emotional distress and support their children effectively at home. Student leadership opportunities, such as participation in the Equity & Wellness Leadership Group, will empower students to contribute to a positive school culture and support their peers. Through these integrated supports, P.S. 007 will create a more inclusive, emotionally safe learning environment that proactively and equitably meets students’ needs. Be sure to indicate how action steps will be introduced and rolled out. Action Steps Implement Weekly Social-Emotional Learning (SEL) Lessons Across All Grades Teachers will facilitate structured SEL morning meetings using programs like Second Step and Sanford Harmony to build students’ emotional awareness, conflict resolution skills, and empathy, helping to reduce behavioral incidents and foster a positive classroom environment. Additional components include: Staff Professional Development: Educators will participate in ongoing training on SEL implementation, trauma-informed practices, and strategies for integrating SEL into daily instruction to ensure fidelity and effectiveness. Family Engagement Workshops: Workshops will be offered to families to increase understanding of SEL concepts, provide tools to support social-emotional growth at home, and strengthen home- school connections. Student Leadership Initiatives: Student leadership groups will help reinforce SEL themes by organizing peer mentoring, leading classroom activities, and promoting a culture of respect and empathy throughout the school. These combined efforts will support the development of a safe, supportive, and emotionally intelligent school community. Launch an MTSS Behavior Support System The school will establish a behavior-focused Multi-Tiered System of Supports (MTSS) to identify and support students with repeated behavioral challenges through check-ins, small group counseling, and regular progress monitoring by the SEL team. Additional components include: Staff Professional Development: All staff will receive training in restorative practices, trauma-informed approaches, and de-escalation strategies to ensure consistent, effective behavioral support across classrooms. Family Engagement Workshops: The school will offer workshops for families focused on understanding behavioral challenges, promoting social-emotional wellness at home, and partnering with the school to support student success. Student Leadership Initiatives: Student groups, such as the Equity & Wellness Leadership Group, will be empowered to lead peer support DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Human Resources P.S. 007 will leverage the expertise of guidance counselors, social workers, the assistant principal, and classroom teachers to implement SEL lessons, restorative practices, and targeted interventions. The SEL team and MTSS coordinators will dedicate time for regular data review and progress monitoring to ensure ongoing responsiveness to student needs. Additionally, partnerships with external mental health providers and district equity specialists will be intentionally integrated into school routines and professional development. Mental health providers will collaborate with staff through co-facilitated training sessions, classroom consultations, and participation in MTSS meetings to align interventions with students’ social-emotional needs. District equity specialists will support staff professional learning focused on culturally responsive practices and equity-centered restorative approaches, reinforcing the school’s commitment to an inclusive and supportive environment. These collaborations will also extend to family engagement efforts, providing specialized workshops and resources that complement school-based supports and promote holistic student well-being. Instructional Resources Materials for Respect for All Week and anti-bullying campaigns, including print resources and multimedia, will be provided to support awareness and positive school culture. To monitor progress, student perception surveys will be administered using tools such as Panorama Education or Google Forms, allowing for consistent collection of feedback on topics like bullying, school climate, and social-emotional learning. For tracking MTSS interventions and student progress, the school will utilize the SWIS (School-Wide Information System) platform, ensuring systematic documentation of behavioral data, attendance, and intervention outcomes to guide decision-making and support. Schedule Adjustments Time will be explicitly allocated within the weekly schedule for SEL morning meetings and monthly guidance lessons focused on bullying prevention and social-emotional skills, ensuring these remain protected and prioritized throughout the school year. Additional time during staff professional development days will be reserved for ongoing training on restorative practices, trauma-informed instruction, and effective SEL implementation to build staff capacity and consistency. Flexible scheduling will be maintained to accommodate small group counseling and check-in/check-out supports without disrupting core instructional time. To support consistent practice, staff will receive coaching and feedback through instructional walkthroughs, reflection sessions, and collaborative planning, fostering a shared commitment to a positive and supportive school climate. Simeone 59 of 283 Priority 3 Action Plan Directions: Identify the key strategy and progressive action steps your school will take to address the specific priority needs and root causes identified for this goal(s). More than one strategy can be indicated if multiple and/or differentiated needs for this priority have been identified. Key strategies should be key levers for success and may represent something that is new to the school. Action steps to operationalize key strategies should focus on new activities or initiatives and/or modifications to current initiatives (e.g., expanded to reach a wider audience or refined for greater impact on students). Note: Routine activities your school does every year should not be included. Key Strategy 1 (Required): By June 2026, PS7 will implement targeted instructional strategies and support systems to strengthen Tier I math instruction for all students. Teachers will receive professional development on effective, CRSE-aligned math instruction and strategies for differentiating instruction to meet the diverse needs of learners. The school will use data from Math MAP Screener assessments and interim math assessments to identify students who need additional support, providing timely interventions through MTSS, including small group math instruction and differentiated resources. Collaboration among classroom teachers, math specialists, and families will be emphasized to ensure that math instruction is culturally relevant, accessible, and aligned with students’ academic needs. Regular progress monitoring using the Math MAP Screener will inform instructional adjustments and guide the closing of achievement gaps in mathematics for all students. Root Cause Addressed: The root cause of the gap in math proficiency stems from inconsistent implementation of high-quality Tier I math instruction across classrooms. This includes limited teacher capacity to effectively differentiate math instruction to meet the diverse needs of all students and insufficient use of data to guide instructional decisions. As a result, students are not receiving the targeted, rigorous math instruction necessary for academic success. There is also a need to strengthen the integration of culturally relevant and sustaining education (CRSE) practices in the math curriculum to ensure all students, regardless of background, are fully engaged and supported in their learning. Simeone 60 of 283 Provide Professional Development on CRSE-Aligned Math Instruction and Differentiation Offer ongoing professional development for math teachers focused on integrating Culturally Responsive and Sustaining Education (CRSE) practices into math instruction. Training will emphasize differentiating math lessons to meet the needs of diverse learners, including strategies for adapting content, processes, and assessments. Teachers will also receive guidance on creating culturally relevant math tasks that engage students and reflect diverse perspectives. Establish Co-Planning and Coaching Cycles for Math Teachers Set up structured co-planning or coaching cycles for math teachers to collaboratively analyze student data, plan targeted small-group math instruction, and design differentiated math tasks. These sessions will support teachers in using formative data (e.g., Math MAP Screener) to identify student needs and adjust instruction to close learning gaps. Regular collaboration will ensure consistent application of high-quality, data-driven math instruction. Implement MTSS and Intervention Strategies for Math Proficiency Gaps Develop and implement a Multi-Tiered System of Supports (MTSS) focused on math proficiency. Teachers will utilize data from assessments, including the Math MAP Screener, to identify students requiring additional support. MTSS strategies will include small-group instruction, targeted interventions, and the use of digital tools or resources designed to address specific math skills and concepts. Teachers will be provided with ongoing support to ensure interventions are effectively addressing math proficiency gaps. Simeone 61 of 283 Implementation Monitoring: Indicate your primary structures to progress monitor implementation of key strategies and action steps. Include specific data/evidence you will collect and use to determine progress and impact on instruction, student learning, and achievement. Note: Implementation Progress Checks will be conducted in the Progress Reporting Tool (PRT) in the iPlan Portal for the 2025-26 school year. To effectively monitor the implementation of strategies aimed at providing all students with a high-quality mathematics academic experience, PS7 will utilize a variety of structures and data sources. One key component of this monitoring will be the regular tracking of student performance on math assessments, including the MAP Math Screener and NYS Math Exam. These assessments will provide valuable data on the progress of all students, with specific targets for different groups. For instance, the percentage of all students performing at or above grade level proficiency is expected to increase by 5% (from 36% to 41%) by June 2026, while students with disabilities (SWD) will see a similar 5% increase (from 33% to 38%), and English language learners (ELL) will also aim for a 5% increase (from 30% to 35%). The data from these assessments will be analyzed quarterly to assess overall growth, identify trends, and track whether instructional strategies are effectively supporting student achievement. Classroom walkthroughs will also be a critical part of the monitoring process. These walkthroughs will be conducted regularly by instructional leaders to assess the fidelity of math instructional shifts, specifically focusing on how well teachers are implementing Culturally Responsive and Sustaining Education (CRSE) practices and differentiating math instruction to meet the needs of diverse learners. The walkthroughs will focus on the integration of CRSE- aligned math tasks, small-group math instruction, and differentiation strategies. A rubric will guide these observations, allowing leaders to provide feedback to teachers and ensure that math lessons are both inclusive and responsive to the needs of all students. The goal is to ensure that all students, regardless of background, have access to engaging and rigorous math instruction. Additionally, PS7 will monitor the effectiveness of MTSS (Multi-Tiered System of Supports) and small-group interventions aimed at addressing math proficiency gaps. Data from these interventions will be collected regularly to track the progress of students receiving targeted support. This includes monitoring Tier 2 and Tier 3 interventions designed for students who are struggling with math skills. Teachers and interventionists will track student progress through pre- and post-assessments, adjusting interventions as necessary to ensure that students are moving toward grade-level proficiency. This data will be particularly important for monitoring progress among students with disabilities (SWD) and English language learners (ELL), who may require more targeted interventions to close achievement gaps. To ensure that instruction is continuously improving, PS7 will also engage in regular data review meetings. These meetings will involve instructional leaders, math coaches, and classroom teachers analyzing data from the MAP Math Screener, classroom observations, and small-group interventions. The purpose of these meetings will be to assess whether instructional practices are effectively supporting student growth and to make necessary adjustments. Teachers will collaborate to refine instructional strategies, share best practices, and align their teaching with the school’s CRSE-aligned math goals. These ongoing discussions will allow the school to be responsive to student needs and ensure that instructional adjustments are made to optimize student success. In summary, PS7’s monitoring plan will focus on tracking student performance data, conducting regular classroom walkthroughs, using data from MTSS interventions, and engaging in ongoing collaborative data reviews. These efforts will allow the school to closely monitor progress toward its SMART goals and ensure that all students, including SWD and ELL students, receive high-quality math instruction that supports their growth and achievement. To address specific learning gaps, small-group intervention blocks and additional math intervention resources will be provided. Scheduled collaborative planning time will ensure continuous instructional improvements, while technology tools and differentiation resources will support diverse learners. Instructional Resources To support the implementation of this action plan and achieve the annual math proficiency goal, PS7 will leverage key resources including math teachers, specialists, and instructional coaches to deliver targeted support and professional development on effective math instruction. The core math curriculum (EnVisions Math) will guide daily instruction, while NWEA MAP Math assessments will be used to track student progress and identify areas for targeted intervention. Teachers will participate in professional development focused on best practices for math instruction, differentiation, and data-driven teaching. Math intervention tools and resources will be used to provide small-group instruction, particularly for students who are struggling to meet grade-level expectations. Collaborative planning time will be allocated for teachers to analyze data, adjust instruction, and ensure alignment with math goals. Additionally, digital math tools and resources for differentiated instruction will support diverse learners and help bridge achievement gaps. Schedule Adjustments To support the implementation of this action plan, PS7 will make key schedule adjustments to ensure that math instruction is effective and targeted to student needs. Dedicated planning time will be scheduled for math teachers to collaborate with instructional coaches and specialists, focusing on analyzing student data from NWEA MAP Math assessments and planning differentiated math lessons. Small-group intervention blocks will be built into the schedule to provide additional support for students who need targeted instruction to close math proficiency gaps. This will include Tier 2 and Tier 3 interventions, with specific time allocated for students to receive focused math instruction. Additionally, teachers will have time to engage in co-planning cycles to ensure alignment with the curriculum and adjust instruction based on student progress. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 92 of 283 Monitoring and Adjustments: Progress will be monitored monthly through attendance records, family engagement logs, and intervention tracking. Root Cause Addressed: The root causes of chronic absenteeism among Students in Temporary Housing (STH) at PS7Q—including unstable living situations, lack of transportation, unmet basic needs, and limited family engagement—will be addressed through a comprehensive, multi-tiered support system. A dedicated attendance team will monitor attendance data and conduct early, personalized outreach to families to identify and resolve barriers to consistent attendance. Collaboration with community partners will provide wraparound supports such as transportation assistance, counseling, and access to basic needs, mitigating external challenges that impact attendance. Strengthening family engagement through communication in preferred languages and culturally responsive workshops will build trust and foster participation. Additionally, professional development for staff on trauma-informed and culturally responsive practices will equip educators to meet the unique needs of STH students, creating a more inclusive and supportive school environment that promotes sustained improvements in attendance and academic engagement. Simeone 124 of 283 High Quality Inclusive & Accessible Learning for Students with Disabilities Action Plan Key Strategy 1 (Required): To promote equity in school discipline at PS7Q, the school will implement a restorative, inclusive, and culturally responsive approach to address disparities affecting historically marginalized student groups, including Black, Latinx, and students with IEPs. Recognizing that disciplinary policies and practices can disproportionately affect certain populations, the school will proactively build staff capacity, embed culturally responsive education (CRE) principles into all behavior support training, and reshape school culture around fairness, reflection, and support. The action plan begins with sustained professional development for all staff, focusing on culturally responsive classroom management, implicit bias, trauma-informed care, SDI-focused interventions, and transition planning workshops to support postsecondary readiness and compliance. These sessions equip educators to respond to student behavior in a supportive, equitable, and data-informed manner. PS7Q will fully integrate restorative practices into daily school routines, including community circles, peer mediation, and structured alternatives to suspension, such as reflection spaces. These practices encourage students to take accountability, repair harm, and strengthen relationships. To ensure fidelity of implementation, the school will utilize coaching logs, walkthroughs, and staff self-assessments, with ongoing support from instructional leaders. The Equity and Inclusion Team will meet monthly to review disaggregated discipline data, monitor implementation of evidence-based behavior supports, identify trends, and guide responsive action steps that link directly to the SMART goal, including reducing disproportionality in suspensions and increasing the use of FBAs/BIPs. Student voice will be amplified through a student leadership council that collaborates with staff to co-create expectations and inform restorative and inclusive policies. Families will be engaged via quarterly multilingual forums, home visits, and ongoing communication to ensure transparency, build trust, and reinforce shared behavioral expectations. Through these comprehensive, culturally responsive, and data-driven strategies, PS7Q aims to create a learning environment where all students feel safe, respected, and empowered to succeed academically and socially, while measurable outcomes reflect progress toward reducing disciplinary inequities. Root Cause Addressed: The root cause of disproportionate disciplinary outcomes at PS7Q—stemming from implicit bias, inconsistent application of behavior policies, and a lack of culturally responsive practices—is being addressed through a comprehensive, equity-focused approach. The school is investing in ongoing professional development to build staff capacity in recognizing and interrupting bias, while promoting culturally responsive classroom management and restorative practices. By embedding daily community-building circles and peer mediation, PS7Q fosters a more inclusive and reflective school climate that emphasizes dialogue and accountability over exclusionary discipline. An Equity & Inclusion Team regularly reviews discipline data to identify trends and ensure interventions effectively reduce disproportionality. Additionally, student leadership councils and multilingual family forums actively engage the community, allowing their voices to shape discipline policies and support systems. These coordinated efforts DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 125 of 283 work together to transform school culture, align discipline practices with equity and inclusion principles, and ultimately create a fairer, more supportive environment for all students. Action Steps: Indicate what your school will do specifically to implement each strategy to achieve this annual goal. Include both student-facing activities and professional learning for staff. Be sure to indicate how action steps will be introduced and rolled out. Action Steps Provide ongoing, evidence-based professional development for all staff using NYC DOE-endorsed programs and strategies, including Restorative Circles, and Positive Behavioral Interventions and Supports (PBIS). Training will focus on implicit bias awareness, culturally responsive classroom management, trauma-informed practices, and restorative justice approaches to promote equitable and consistent discipline implementation. PD will be introduced and sustained through coaching cycles, turnkey sessions, and embedded DOE PD days, with ongoing observation, feedback, and reflection opportunities to ensure staff integrate these practices into daily routines and schoolwide behavior supports. Establish a regular review process led by the Equity & Inclusion Team to analyze disaggregated discipline data, identify disproportionality trends, and develop targeted interventions. Reviews will occur monthly, with outcomes informing both instructional supports and policy adjustments. The process will actively involve student leaders and families, capturing their input through surveys, forums, and focus groups to ensure policies reflect student and community perspectives. Findings will guide responsive, culturally informed interventions and be tracked to measure progress toward SMART goals, including reductions in suspension disparities and increased use of FBAs/BIPs. Note: Implementation Progress Checks will be conducted in the Progress Reporting Tool (PRT) in the iPlan Portal for the 2025-26 school year. Monitoring will be conducted through monthly Equity & Inclusion Team meetings, where discipline data disaggregated by race, disability status, and other demographics will be reviewed. The school will track number and types of disciplinary incidents, suspension rates, referrals, and the frequency of restorative justice practices to assess both outcomes and fidelity of implementation. Fidelity will also be monitored through walkthroughs, staff self-assessments, and coaching logs to ensure consistent application of evidence-based behavior supports. In addition, feedback from teacher surveys and student focus groups will be collected to evaluate classroom climate, perceptions of fairness, and the effectiveness of supports. This qualitative input, combined with quantitative data, will inform adjustments to professional development, instructional practices, and behavior interventions, reinforcing equitable outcomes. The monitoring process will also ensure that procedural safeguards and inclusive practices, such as least restrictive environments and culturally responsive supports, are consistently reflected in IEPs and discipline decisions. Simeone 129 of 283 High Quality Inclusive & Accessible Learning for Students with Disabilities Budget and Resources Budget and Resources Directions: Indicate available and anticipated resources that will be leveraged to achieve this annual goal and implement this action plan, including human resources, instructional resources (e.g., curricula, programs), schedule adjustments, other. Use N/A if not applicable and/or indicate other resource needs. Schools are encouraged to think critically and strategically when considering resources that can support progress towards CEP goals for students with IEPs. This includes utilizing Title I funding for at-risk speech and/or counseling services, all-staff training in supporting students with autism, sensory materials, summer institute geared at the development of executive functioning skills, and/or inclusion coaching support. Schools may also consider utilizing Title II funds to reduce the ratio of students with IEPs to typically developing peers in ICT classes. Human Resources: PS7Q will strategically leverage special education teachers, inclusion coaches, school counselors, speech therapists, and trained autism support staff to ensure high-fidelity implementation of IEP development, transition planning, and behavior supports. ENL providers and paraprofessionals will be deployed to support multilingual learners, facilitate small-group interventions, and reinforce differentiated instruction aligned to student needs. Additionally, Title I and Title II funded staff will help reduce student-to-teacher ratios, provide targeted academic and behavioral interventions, and monitor progress to ensure students receive timely and appropriate support. This coordinated human capital strategy ensures that each staff member’s expertise is directly linked to the school’s Priority 4 and Priority 5 goals, promoting equity and consistent outcomes for students with IEPs, students in temporary housing, and multilingual learners. Instructional Resources PS7Q will utilize curricula and programs specifically designed to support executive functioning, sensory regulation, and assistive technology, ensuring alignment with inclusive and culturally responsive practices. Additionally, professional development materials and resources will be provided for all staff to strengthen their capacity in supporting diverse learners, implementing SDI strategies, and delivering high-quality, individualized instruction. These resources work in tandem with human capital and scheduling structures to ensure consistent, effective support for students with disabilities across academic, behavioral, and transition planning contexts. Schedule Adjustments Schedule modifications will include dedicated time for inclusion coaching, collaborative planning among general and special education staff, and blocks allocated for targeted interventions and counseling services. Other Resources Needed DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 130 of 283 Additional funding may be required to fully implement the action plan, including support for summer institutes focused on executive functioning, expanded access to culturally responsive professional development, and resources to strengthen family engagement in IEP processes. PS7Q will leverage partnerships with local community- based organizations (CBOs) such as Advocates for Children, Make the Road NY, and The Door, as well as external professional learning providers, to support family outreach, provide wraparound student services, and deliver specialized staff training. These partnerships ensure that resources are directly aligned to the school’s strategies and support equitable access for students with disabilities, multilingual learners, and historically underserved populations. Simeone 131 of 283 Section 6: Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) Section 6: Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) Academic Support NYCPS Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) align with NYSED guidelines for providing Academic Intervention Services (AIS) to students who have not yet met state standards or are considered to be at- risk of not meeting state standards. The Multi-tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) framework, organizes supports into three tiers: Tier 1 provides schoolwide, high-quality programming for all students, including SWDs; Tier 2 offers data informed small-group interventions for at-promise students; and Tier 3 delivers intensive, individualized support for students with persistent needs. This facilitates the strategic planning and necessary resource alignment to ensure their school community appropriately and adequately serves all students well, in partnership with students and families. MTSS identifies the academic support services to be provided and ensures that students receive Response to Intervention (RtI) and Positive Behavioral Interventions and Support (PBIS) strategies as needed. See NYSED’s memo to view the criteria for determining AIS services. The Supportive Environment Framework (SEF) offers a wide range of evidence-based Tier 2 and Tier 3 interventions and supports for student social-emotional, behavioral, physical and mental wellness, with particular attention to the unique needs of students in Temporary Housing and/or Foster Care, who may face additional challenges related to school stability, trauma and access to educational resources. A culturally responsive-sustaining approach to intervention embeds culturally relevant texts and historical narratives into literacy and social studies interventions, ensuring students receive the support that reflects their identities and lived experiences, increasing engagement and achievement. Use the charts below and a Multi-Tiered System of Support (MTSS) to match academic, social/emotional, behavioral and instructional resources with student needs to provide targeted support for all learners, especially at-risk students who experience difficulty meeting state standards, including those living in Temporary Housing and/or residing in Foster Care placements. Indicate specialized materials, technology- based tools and/or extra instruction, including Expanded Learning Time that supplement school-wide systems & supports and/or classroom level supports. Ensure that interventions address the unique social- emotional, behavioral and academic challenges faced by culturally and linguistically diverse students, as well as those impacted by housing instability and child welfare system involvement. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 134 of 283 Family and Community Engagement Around MTSS Describe how the school’s essential components of MTSS are shared with families. PS7Q ensures that families are actively informed and involved in the school’s Multi-Tiered System of Supports (MTSS). The essential components of MTSS—including Tier 1 universal supports, Tier 2 small-group interventions, and Tier 3 individualized services—are shared with families through multilingual newsletters, family workshops, and individualized meetings. Families receive clear explanations of intervention criteria, progress monitoring methods, and expected outcomes, with opportunities to provide input and co-design supports for their children. Outreach strategies include phone calls, home visits, and digital platforms to ensure consistent communication with families of multilingual learners, Students in Temporary Housing (STH), foster care students, and other high-need populations. These efforts aim to increase family understanding of MTSS, foster engagement in intervention planning, and support student success through collaborative partnerships. Describe how families receive updates on the progress of their child(ren) who receive Tier 2 targeted, or Tier 3 intensive interventions. At PS7Q, families of students receiving Tier 2 targeted or Tier 3 intensive interventions are regularly updated through multiple, consistent communication channels. In addition to the two scheduled parent-teacher conferences each year, families receive monthly or quarterly progress updates via phone calls, emails, virtual meetings, or in- person check-ins. These updates include data from specific intervention tools and assessments, such as academic benchmark assessments, social-emotional behavior tracking, and progress monitoring logs, which are shared in accessible and multilingual formats. Teachers, intervention specialists, and school counselors work with families to interpret the data and provide guidance on how to reinforce learning and social-emotional growth at home. Individualized meetings ensure families can ask questions, provide input, and co-plan next steps when adjustments to interventions are needed. This structured and ongoing communication strengthens the home-school partnership, fosters transparency, and empowers families to actively support their child’s progress within the Tier 2 and Tier 3 MTSS framework. Describe how families are involved during decision making regarding the progress of students receiving Tier 3 intensive intervention. At PS7Q, families play a central role in the decision-making process for students receiving Tier 3 intensive interventions. Families are engaged as active partners through structured, recurring meetings, which occur monthly or as needed depending on student progress. These meetings include teachers, intervention specialists, counselors, school leaders, and when appropriate, special education liaisons. During each session, student progress data—collected through benchmark assessments, progress monitoring tools, and behavioral/social-emotional tracking systems—is reviewed collaboratively. Families are supported in interpreting the data, providing observations, and contributing insights that guide the adjustment of individualized intervention plans. Staff roles are clearly defined: teachers and intervention specialists present academic and social- emotional data, counselors provide support around student well-being, and school leaders facilitate decisions to ensure alignment with MTSS frameworks and IEP goals. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 135 of 283 PS7Q further empowers families through workshops, informational sessions, and multilingual communication, enabling them to understand intervention goals, ask questions, and actively participate in strategies that support their child’s academic and social-emotional growth. This collaborative, structured approach strengthens family- school partnerships, reinforces accountability, and ensures Tier 3 interventions are both responsive and effective for all students. After analyzing historical demographic, attendance, and achievement data for students in foster care, what trends have emerged? After analyzing historical demographic, attendance, and achievement data for students in foster care at PS7Q, several trends have emerged. These students often experience higher rates of chronic absenteeism compared to their peers, largely due to frequent placement changes and related disruptions. Academic performance data indicates that students in foster care may fall behind grade-level expectations, particularly in literacy and math, reflecting the compounded effects of instability and trauma. Social-emotional indicators also demonstrate increased need for counseling, behavioral supports, and interventions tailored to trauma-informed care. To address these trends, PS7Q implements targeted interventions that are monitored for effectiveness using multiple tools and protocols. Attendance dashboards, academic progress reports, benchmark assessments, and individualized progress trackers are reviewed regularly—typically on a monthly basis—by counselors, social workers, and intervention teams. This data informs adjustments to individualized plans, ensuring timely and responsive support. Staff receive ongoing training to recognize and respond to the unique needs of newly transitioning students, including trauma-informed practices, restorative approaches, and culturally responsive strategies. Cross-agency collaboration with foster care providers ensures immediate re-enrollment, transportation support, and continuity of services, minimizing the impact of placement changes on student learning. By combining data-informed decision-making, staff professional development, and strong interagency collaboration, PS7Q fosters a responsive and inclusive environment that promotes academic growth, social-emotional well-being, and long-term success for students in foster care. Simeone 142 of 283 Section 7: Parent and Family Engagement Policy and School-Parent Compact Section 7: Parent and Family Engagement Policy and School-Parent Compact Parent and Family Engagement Policy Directions: The Parent and Family Engagement Policy should describe how your school will plan and implement effective parent and family engagement activities and/or strategies to improve student academic achievement and school performance. The School-Parent Compact (SPC) is a component of the Parent and Family Engagement Policy (PFEP) that outlines how parents, the entire school staff, and students will share this responsibility. The activities and/or strategies included in your school's Parent and Family Engagement Policy should align with current Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP) goals for improving student achievement and should be developed or updated with input and feedback from parent leaders and the Parent Coordinator. In addition, if the school community will be engaged in central parent engagement initiatives, such as Parent Academy, which will provide training for school communities to help strengthen family-school partnerships, please be sure to include these activities in the school's policy. In Title I schools, the Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) represents the Title I parent community in supporting parent and family engagement. The Title I PAC Chairperson will serve in a consultative role with the SLT representing the school’s Title I parents. Schools are encouraged to use the sample Parent and Family Engagement and SPC templates below (which meet federal Title I parent and family engagement requirements) as guidance for updating the school's current policy. Simeone 146 of 283 School-Parent Compact (SPC) School-Parent Compact (SPC) P.S. 7Q, in compliance with Section 1116 of Title I, Part A of the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), implements a School-Parent Compact to strengthen partnerships that support student achievement. The Compact clearly defines the shared responsibility of parents, students, and school staff in promoting academic success and ensuring all students meet State Standards. Shared Responsibilities: Parents: Support learning at home by reviewing assignments, attending parent workshops, monitoring progress through communication with teachers, and participating in decision-making through advisory councils and conferences. Students: Take responsibility for completing assignments, actively participating in class, setting personal learning goals, and engaging in self-assessment and reflection. School Staff: Provide high-quality instruction aligned to standards, offer targeted interventions, maintain open communication with families, and provide regular updates on academic progress. Collaborative Review & Updates: The Compact is reviewed and updated annually with input from parents, students, and staff during School Leadership Team meetings, Parent Association discussions, and Title I advisory council sessions. Feedback collected from surveys, conferences, and workshops informs adjustments to the Compact to better reflect evolving academic goals, family needs, and CEP priorities. This ongoing collaboration ensures alignment between the Compact, Title I objectives, and school-wide strategies for improving student outcomes. By outlining clear roles and responsibilities and fostering continuous collaboration, P.S. 7Q promotes a unified approach to student success that actively engages families, empowers students, and strengthens instructional support. Simeone 150 of 283 Title I Program Information Title I Program Information Part 1: Title I Program Type Title I Requirements and Strengthening Title I Parent Involvement: Under USDOE’s Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), Title I schools are required to address professional learning and parent and family engagement in the Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP). To strengthen parent leadership in Title I schools, and increase the involvement of all parents of Title I eligible children in improving students’ academic outcomes, all Title I schools are required to establish a Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) that will serve as the consultative and representative body for all Title I parents in the school to provide voice into the spending of Title I funds. The SLT is responsible for facilitating consultation with the Title I Parent Advisory Council regarding the joint development of the CEP, Parent and Family Engagement Policy, School Parent Compact (SPC) and the use of the 1% parent involvement set-aside in Title I schools. The Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) Chairperson (or alternate) is expected to attend all regular meetings of the School Leadership Team (SLT). Title I parents, as represented by the Title I PAC Chairperson, must be consulted regarding the use of the Title I 1% set-aside for parent and family engagement. The Title I 1% set-aside funding is used to supplement parent and family engagement activities as described in the school’s Parent and Family Engagement Policy and must be aligned with student achievement goals as outlined in the CEP. For additional guidance on the Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC), go to the Protocols and Timeline for Title I Parent and Family Engagement Activities document. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 157 of 283 Explanation/Background 1. Explanation/Background: Title I Schoolwide Program schools are expected to use the flexibility available to them to integrate services and programs with the aim of enhancing the entire educational program and helping all students reach proficient and advanced levels of achievement. In addition to coordinating and integrating services, Schoolwide Program schools may combine most Federal, State and local funds to provide those services. By consolidating funds from Federal, State, and local sources, a Schoolwide Program school can address its needs using all its available resources. This gives a school more flexibility in how it uses available resources to meet the identified needs of all its students. Consolidating funds in a Schoolwide Program means that a school treats the funds it is consolidating like they are a single “pool” of funds. In other words, the funds from the contributing programs in the school lose their individual identity and the school has one flexible pool of funds. The school uses funds from this consolidated Schoolwide pool to support any activity of the Schoolwide Program without regard to which program contributed the specific funds used for a particular activity – so long as the use of funds meets the intent and purposes of the programs from which the funds originate. To consolidate funding in a Schoolwide Program, the school does not literally need to combine funds in a single account or pool with its own accounting code. Rather, the word “pool” is used conceptually to convey that a Schoolwide Program school has the use of all consolidated funds available to it for the dedicated function of operating a Schoolwide Program without regard to the identity of those funds. Consolidating Federal funds in a Schoolwide Program has the following additional advantages: Consolidating Federal funds eases the requirements for accounting for funds from each specific program separately, because a Schoolwide school is not required to distinguish among funds received from different sources when accounting for their use. A school that consolidates Federal funds in its Schoolwide Program is not required to meet most of the statutory and regulatory requirements of the specific Federal programs included in the consolidation (e.g., semi-annual time and effort reporting for Title I). However, the school must ensure that it meets the intent and purposes of the Federal programs included in the consolidation so that the needs of the intended beneficiaries are met. 2. The intent and purposes of the Federal programs indicated on the chart above (Part 4c of this section) are as follows: Title I, Part A – Schoolwide Programs: To upgrade the entire educational program in the school in order to improve the academic achievement of all students, particularly the lowest-achieving students. This includes provision of services for Students in Temporary Housing (STH). Simeone 158 of 283 Support implementation of school improvement plans that aims to improve instruction and address the identified needs Title II, Part A: Supplementary funding to improve student academic achievement by reducing class size in grades K, 1, 2, and 3, with an emphasis on grades with an average register greater than 20. If space is not available to form additional classes, funds may support push-in teacher(s) to supplement the instructional program. For schools that choose to reduce class size, priority must be given to reducing class size in general education settings and must demonstrate the following: Reduce class sizes to 15-18 students or fewer (including the use of co-teaching and floating teachers that bring the student to teacher ratio to one teacher per 15-18 students); Be accompanied by a rigorous curriculum, effective teachers, and a sustained, job-embedded professional development plan. Title III, Part A: To help ensure that children with limited English proficiency become proficient in English, develop high academic attainment in English, and meet the same challenging State academic content and achievement standards in the core academic subjects that all other children are expected to meet. Another purpose of this program is to increase the capacity of schools to establish, implement and sustain high-quality language instruction programs and English language development programs that assist schools in effectively teaching students with limited English proficiency. Title III, Part A is also designed to promote the participation of parents and communities of limited English proficient children in English language instruction programs. Title III Immigrant: Supplementary and enhanced services to immigrant students, in the areas of English language acquisition and content area achievement. Immigrant children and youth are defined as individuals who are aged 3 through 21, have not been attending school in any one or more states for more than three (3) full academic years, and were not born in any state. “State” means the 50 states of the United States, the District of Columbia, and the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico. Children born to U.S. citizens abroad, the U.S. Virgin Islands, or any other U.S. territory that is not D.C. or Puerto Rico are to be considered immigrants. Title IV, Part A: Title IV funds are for schools that are among those with the greatest needs, have the highest numbers of students from low-income families, are identified for CS, ATSI or TSI, or are identified as persistently dangerous public schools. Allowable uses of funds under well-rounded education, safety and health, and educational technology include direct services for students, professional development for teachers and administrators, and supplemental educational resources.
