Simeone 171 of 283 Describe your English as a New Language (ENL), and if applicable Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE), and Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) programs. For each program, include: How stand-alone ENL is implemented as per CR Part 154 How integrated ENL is implemented as per CR Part 154 How students are grouped (e.g., block [class travels together as a group], ungraded [all students regardless of grade are in one class], heterogeneous [mixed proficiency levels], homogenous [proficiency level is the same in one class]). a. English as a New Language (ENL) Stand-alone ENL: Our English as a New Language (ENL) program in grades K–5 provides a comprehensive combination of integrated and stand-alone instruction that is fully aligned with CR Part 154 requirements. This ensures that every English Language Learner receives the mandated instructional minutes, is taught by appropriately certified ENL staff, and participates in curriculum-aligned lessons designed to promote both language development and academic success. Our current ELL population includes 349 students across all proficiency levels, including 20 Entering, 60 Emerging, 97 Transitioning, 123 Expanding, and 49 Commanding students who receive continued support during their two-year extension period. Stand-alone ENL services are provided by grade level, with 63 kindergarten, 64 first-grade, 82 second-grade, 82 third-grade, 63 fourth-grade, and 43 fifth-grade students receiving direct, proficiency-based instruction. Integrated ENL instruction is delivered through a co-teaching model in which ENL-certified teachers push into general education classrooms to support English Language Arts instruction using the HMH Into Reading curriculum. This approach satisfies CR Part 154 by embedding language development directly into grade-level content with a certified ENL teacher present. During these lessons, ENL teachers provide scaffolds such as visual supports, sentence frames, bilingual glossaries, and explicit vocabulary instruction, ensuring that students at all proficiency levels can meaningfully access the curriculum. For example, in a Grade 4 literacy block, an ENL teacher rotates through small-group literacy stations alongside the classroom teacher, supporting Entering through Expanding-level students during reading comprehension and vocabulary tasks. These heterogeneous groupings allow ELLs to work collaboratively with peers of varying proficiency levels, benefiting from rich language exposure and peer modeling. Stand-alone ENL instruction complements the integrated model by providing focused, proficiency-level instruction that occurs separately from the core ELA block, in full compliance with CR Part 154 requirements. These sessions are taught by ENL-certified teachers and are scheduled according to each student’s mandated minutes. Stand-alone instruction uses the Rigby Guided Reading program and language-focused lessons that target foundational literacy, oral language development, vocabulary building, grammar, and reading comprehension. In these settings, grouping is primarily homogeneous. For instance, a group of Entering-level Grade 2 students may work together in a small group to build oral language, practice phonics, and strengthen comprehension through structured, level-appropriate texts. Across the school, we maintain 16 homogeneous groups for stand-alone instruction and support 12 heterogeneous classes through integrated co-teaching. Vocabulary development is intentionally embedded across both instructional models. ENL teachers pre-teach academic vocabulary aligned with upcoming content, helping students build the background knowledge necessary to fully engage with new material. Through visuals, concrete examples, hands-on activities, and explicit instruction in morphology—including prefixes, roots, and suffixes—students develop both the conceptual understanding, and the language structures needed for academic success. Together, the integration of co-taught lessons, targeted stand-alone sessions, structured grouping practices, and explicit vocabulary instruction creates a strong, cohesive ENL program. This approach ensures that students receive comprehensive language support, consistent access to grade-level standards, and targeted instruction based on DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 172 of 283 their individual proficiency needs. As a result, our ELLs are well-prepared to make measurable progress in both English language acquisition and overall academic achievement. Integrated ENL: In grades K–5, our school offers 18 self-contained English as a New Language (ENL) classes taught by dually certified ENL and Common Branch teachers. These classrooms are intentionally structured as heterogeneous learning environments, bringing together students across all proficiency levels—including Entering, Emerging, Transitioning, Expanding, and Commanding—as well as Non-ELL students. This model allows language development to take place within a natural, interactive setting where students learn from one another while engaging in content- rich lessons. Across the school, we currently serve 349 students, with 20 Entering, 60 Emerging, 97 Transitioning, 123 Expanding, and 49 Commanding students represented. All ENL classes follow the workshop model across content areas, ensuring that instruction is student-centered, interactive, and grounded in differentiated small-group teaching. Because these classes are heterogeneous by design, English Language Learners receive daily opportunities to practice language skills alongside English- proficient peers. In alignment with CR Part 154, Entering and Emerging ELLs receive 180 minutes of stand-alone ENL instruction and 180 minutes of integrated ENL instruction each week. When a classroom teacher holds ENL certification, these 360 minutes may be met entirely through integrated instruction without requiring pull-out services. Transitioning and Expanding ELLs receive 180 minutes of integrated ENL support, while Commanding students receive 90 minutes per week. To meet these requirements, 80 Entering and Emerging students receive their 180 minutes of stand-alone instruction outside the regular classroom. These students are grouped by grade and proficiency level, with 63 kindergarten students, 64 first-grade students, 82 second-grade students, 82 third-grade students, 63 fourth-grade students, and 43 fifth-grade students receiving stand-alone instruction. Transitioning, Expanding, and Commanding students receive their mandated minutes through a combination of push-in and pull-out support, depending on individual student needs and the ENL teacher’s schedule. This service model ensures that all ELLs receive instruction targeted to their proficiency level while remaining active participants in a linguistically diverse classroom environment. By balancing stand-alone instruction with integrated, language-rich learning experiences, we provide students with comprehensive support that strengthens both language acquisition and academic achievement. b. Bilingual Education (If applicable) Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE): N/A Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE): Our Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE) program includes one self-contained one-way Spanish Dual Language class and one self-contained one-way Chinese Dual Language class at every grade level from kindergarten through grade five. The Spanish DLBE program serves a total of 128 students, including 54 English Language Learners (ELLs) distributed as follows: 8 Entering, 15 Emerging, 7 Transitioning, 15 Expanding, and 9 Commanding. The Chinese DLBE program serves a total of 122 students, including 56 ELLs distributed as follows: 2 DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 173 of 283 Entering, 4 Emerging, 7 Transitioning, 20 Expanding, and 23 Commanding. These heterogeneous classes are intentionally structured to promote bilingualism, biliteracy, and cross-linguistic transfer, providing daily opportunities for students to interact across varying levels of English and home-language proficiency. Instruction in both the Spanish and Chinese DLBE programs is delivered through a 50/50 language allocation model. Our K–5 classrooms alternate the language of instruction daily to provide equal access to English and the target language across the week. English literacy instruction is provided through the HMH Into Reading program, while Spanish literacy instruction uses Arriba la Lectura! alongside Estrellita/Lunita for foundational skills. Chinese literacy instruction is delivered through Shared Reading with iChineseReader and the Chinese Made Easy program. Content areas, including Math, Science, and Social Studies, are also divided evenly across a five-day cycle to ensure that students develop academic vocabulary and conceptual understanding in both languages. Within the DLBE model, ENL support is integrated directly into the bilingual classroom. All bilingual teachers are dually certified and deliver the mandated ENL services required under CR Part 154.2. This means that ELLs receive their required English language development minutes through a combination of English-medium instruction, explicit language scaffolds, and targeted ENL strategies embedded within the curriculum. Teachers incorporate vocabulary previews, sentence frames, language objectives, and targeted small-group instruction to ensure that ELLs strengthen their English proficiency while continuing to develop literacy and content knowledge in the partner language. This balanced approach allows bilingual instruction and ENL services to work in tandem, supporting students’ language development in both English and their home language without removing them from the dual- language environment. All bilingual classrooms maintain clear language separation through a consistent color-coding system—red for Spanish or Chinese and blue for English. Classroom libraries, anchor charts, word walls, and instructional materials are labeled accordingly, helping students develop metalinguistic awareness and reinforcing the designated language of instruction for each day. How does your school ensure the mandated number of instructional minutes are provided according to students’ English language proficiency levels in each program model? The organization of our instructional staff ensures that all English Language Learners (ELLs) receive the mandated number of instructional minutes in full alignment with CR Part 154.2. Dual Language teachers are certified in Common Branch with a Bilingual Extension, and our self-contained ENL classes are taught by teachers who hold dual certification in Common Branch and TESOL. Students receive the appropriate number of ENL support units based on their NYSITELL or NYSESLAT proficiency levels, and all ELLs are placed in classes in accordance with the instructional requirements established by the state. Self-contained ENL classes are heterogeneously grouped to include students who require double service, single service, or half service, and they may also include English- proficient model students who support language-rich classroom interactions. In the Free-Standing English as a New Language (ENL) program, students receive all core instruction in English with embedded language supports and scaffolds. Per CR Part 154.2, Entering and Emerging students receive 360 minutes of ENL instruction weekly—180 minutes of stand-alone ENL and one unit of integrated ENL/ELA instruction. Transitioning and Expanding students receive 180 minutes of integrated ENL instruction per week, while Commanding students receive 90 minutes of integrated ENL instruction delivered within the content areas. Instruction across all proficiency levels is differentiated and scaffolded to ensure that students can access grade- level content while developing English proficiency. In the Dual Language program, Home Language Arts instructional minutes are delivered by the bilingual classroom teacher, and all ELLs are purposefully grouped to receive instruction aligned with their proficiency levels. Students in the DLBE model develop English and target-language skills while learning content in both languages. Core content is taught in Spanish/English or Chinese/English by the bilingual classroom teachers, with additional English-language content instruction supported by cluster teachers. In both the Dual Language and Free-Standing ENL models, core content is delivered using instructional approaches that make learning comprehensible and support language development while meeting the demands of the Next Generation Learning Standards. Instruction is differentiated for Entering, Emerging, Transitioning, Expanding, and Commanding students through scaffolding, extensive modeling, and deliberate use of ENL strategies such as activating prior knowledge, total physical response, making personal and cultural connections, and using visuals and realia. Teachers scaffold learning through teacher-led modeling, interactive and shared activities, and supported small-group and independent work, ensuring that all students are able to access rigorous content expectations. Within the Dual Language program, students develop academic skills in their dominant language (L1) and transfer those skills to the target language (L2). English Language Learners and English-proficient peers are fully integrated for all content instruction. During the literacy block, students receive instruction in both languages following a daily alternating pattern. Teachers design lessons that incorporate verbal and nonverbal cues, visual supports, manipulatives, gestures, hands-on materials, and small-group instruction to ensure comprehension. Authentic songs, poems, literature, rhymes, and cultural artifacts are used to build language proficiency and cultural awareness. Teachers integrate listening, speaking, reading, writing, observing, and thinking into meaningful language activities and use a variety of feedback techniques—such as elicitation, repetition, metalinguistic clues, and nonverbal prompts—to support cross-linguistic development. Dual Language teachers also consider students’ developmental needs and proficiency levels when planning instruction, adjusting language demands accordingly. Newly admitted students are paired with classmates who share the same home language for socio-emotional and instructional support, as well as with peers who speak the target language to promote natural language exchange. In side-by-side Dual Language settings, teachers regularly discuss formative assessment outcomes and student progress to inform collaborative planning across classrooms. They develop aligned language and content objectives to clearly define instructional outcomes and ensure coherence in lesson delivery. In the Free-Standing ENL program—through both integrated and stand-alone services—teachers use the SIOP model to make core content comprehensible while advancing English language development. Lessons include clearly defined content and language objectives and incorporate instructional approaches such as adaptive texts, DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 175 of 283 leveled study guides, jigsaw reading, graphic organizers, thinking maps, note-taking supports, and hands-on supplementary materials. These methods enable ELLs to engage with grade-level content while developing the linguistic skills required for academic success. 3. How are ENL, English Language Arts (ELA) and if applicable, Home Language Arts (HLA) instructional minutes delivered in each program model? At P.S. 7Q, ENL, ELA, and HLA instruction is intentionally structured to meet the linguistic and academic needs of English Language Learners at every proficiency level. Our school delivers ENL services through both Stand-Alone and Integrated ENL models, aligned to our curriculum and the strengths of our teaching staff. For students at the Entering and Emerging proficiency levels, our ENL team provides daily Stand-Alone instruction that focuses on foundational language development using scaffolded components of HMH Into Reading, vocabulary routines, and oral language practice aligned to grade-level topics. These students also participate in Integrated ENL during core content instruction, where ENL teachers co-plan and co-teach with classroom teachers to embed language objectives, model academic discourse, and provide structured supports. Students at the Transitioning level receive a combination of Stand-Alone and Integrated ENL instruction. Stand- Alone sessions emphasize reading comprehension, grammar, and academic vocabulary that parallel their HMH literacy units. Integrated ENL pushes language development into the content areas, with ENL teachers supporting sentence frames, small-group instruction, and targeted conferencing. Students at the Expanding and Commanding levels are primarily served through Integrated ENL, where co-teaching ensures that language demands within ELA, math, science, and social studies are explicitly supported. Teachers incorporate strategies such as discussion protocols, vocabulary routines, and text-dependent questioning to maintain high expectations. ELA instruction is delivered by certified classroom teachers using HMH Into Reading across all grades, with ENL teachers supporting language access through scaffolds, previewing/reviewing texts, and small-group reinforcement tailored to proficiency needs. Home Language Arts (HLA) is offered when there is a sufficient number of students who share a common home language. When provided, HLA instruction reinforces literacy skills that transfer between languages, supports biliteracy development, and strengthens students’ comprehension of grade-level content. Together, these structures ensure that every multilingual learner at P.S. 7Q receives language and literacy instruction that is aligned to grade-level curriculum, responsive to their proficiency levels, and supported through collaboration between ENL, general education, and HLA teachers. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 176 of 283 4. For schools with Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE) programs: a. Which Dual Language Bilingual Education model is implemented? Explain how much of the instructional day (percentage) English proficient students and ELLs are integrated. Our self-contained, one-way dual language program serves students in grades K-5, with separate classes for Spanish Dual Language and Chinese Dual Language at each grade level. Each class is heterogeneously mixed, consisting of approximately 50% English Language Learners (ELLs) and 50% English Proficient (EP) students. In these classrooms, the language allocation for both content and literacy instruction is balanced, with approximately 50% of the instruction provided in English and 50% in the target language (either Spanish or Chinese). This balance is designed to promote bilingualism and biliteracy, ensuring that students develop proficiency in both languages while acquiring content knowledge. To meet the requirements of Part 154, ELLs receive the mandated English as a New Language (ENL) services required by CR Part 154.2, delivered by their certified bilingual teachers. These teachers also provide content-area instruction in both languages, supporting students' academic growth in subjects like Math, Science, Social Studies, and English Language Arts (ELA). In our dual language classrooms, students do not have separate language instruction time; instead, they are integrated for 100% of the instructional day, with English-proficient and native- language proficient students learning together. This integration ensures that all students benefit from peer-to-peer learning and develop language skills in both English and the target language. Instruction is designed so that content areas such as Math, Science, and Social Studies are taught using a 50/50 language distribution throughout a 5-day cycle. All dual language classrooms are equipped with instructional materials in both languages and are clearly organized using a color-coding system to designate the language of instruction—Red for the target language (Spanish or Chinese) and Blue for English. This system helps students and teachers easily identify which language is being used at any given time. By adhering to these practices, our program meets the guidelines of Part 154 for dual language instruction, ensuring that all students receive a high-quality, balanced education in both languages while supporting the development of academic and language skills. b. In which language(s) is each core content area taught? In our dual language program, English and Chinese are taught in all core subject areas for the Chinese dual language classes, and English and Spanish are taught in all core subject areas for the Spanish dual language classes. The classroom teacher provides instruction in both languages across Math, Science, Social Studies, and English Language Arts (ELA). For Science, additional instruction is provided in English by a specialized science cluster teacher, ensuring that students receive content-specific support in English while maintaining the balance of bilingual instruction in the core subjects. What is the composition of the ELL subgroups at your school? What percentage of ELLs are Newcomers? How does effective instruction for Newcomers differ from those provided to Developing or Long-Term ELLs? At P.S. 7Q, the English Language Learner (ELL) population is composed of 341 Newcomers (0–3 years of service), representing 85.9% of total ELLs; 56 Developing ELLs (4–6 years of service), representing 14.1%; and currently no Long-Term ELLs (7+ years of service). Instruction is differentiated to meet the needs of each subgroup. For Newcomers, lessons focus on foundational language acquisition, prioritizing listening and speaking before reading and writing. Instruction is highly scaffolded, visual, and context-based, with explicit attention to vocabulary, sentence structures, and cultural integration, often supported through gestures, realia, modeling, and small-group activities. Developing ELLs receive targeted support in academic language, including subject-specific vocabulary, reading comprehension, and writing skills, while continuing to build oral language proficiency. Scaffolded strategies such as graphic organizers, sentence frames, and collaborative activities help bridge content and language learning. Although P.S. 7Q currently has no Long-Term ELLs, this group typically requires advanced support in academic literacy, including complex text analysis, essay writing, and higher-order thinking skills, with an emphasis on refining academic vocabulary across content areas. All ELL instruction is aligned with the New York State ELL Policy and Reference Guide and CR Part 154, ensuring students receive appropriate language support, qualified instruction, ongoing assessment, and access to additional services as needed. Family engagement is prioritized through communication in students’ home languages to promote involvement and support learning. What percentage of ELLs are Students with Inconsistent/Interrupted Formal Education (SIFE)? How do you differentiate effective instruction for SIFE? Students with Inconsistent or Interrupted Formal Education (SIFE) represent 0.3% of the total ELL population. Effective instruction for SIFE students requires a highly differentiated approach that addresses their unique challenges, including gaps in foundational academic knowledge and limited or interrupted exposure to formal schooling. Instruction focuses on building basic literacy and numeracy skills alongside English language DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 179 of 283 development. Teachers provide a language-rich environment that incorporates visuals, manipulatives, hands-on activities, and peer interactions to support comprehension and engagement. Lessons are scaffolded to address varying proficiency levels and educational backgrounds, using strategies such as graphic organizers, sentence starters, modeling, guided practice, and simplified or leveled content. Small-group and one-on-one instruction may be provided to target specific learning gaps, while culturally responsive practices connect new content to students’ prior experiences and knowledge. Frequent formative assessments allow teachers to adjust instruction and provide targeted interventions to ensure foundational skills are reinforced and academic growth is supported. Under Part 154 of New York State’s Education Law, schools are required to appropriately identify SIFE students and provide specialized instruction that supports both language development and academic progress. This includes access to Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) or English as a New Language (ENL) programs, as well as supplementary support services such as counseling or special education when needed. Regular monitoring and assessment ensure SIFE students are making progress, and parental engagement is prioritized, with information and communication provided in students’ home languages to actively support learning at school and at home. What percentage of ELLs are Long-Term ELLs? How does effective instruction for Long-Term ELLs differ from instruction designed for Developing ELLs? In our school community, Long-Term English Language Learners (ELLs) currently make up 0.0% of the student population. If we did have Long-Term ELLs, instruction for these students would differ from that provided to Developing ELLs. Long-Term ELLs are students who have been in the U.S. school system for five or more years and continue to face challenges with English proficiency, particularly in academic language and content mastery. Effective instruction would focus on building academic literacy, enhancing reading comprehension, writing skills, and advanced vocabulary, as these students typically have conversational English but need support with formal, academic language. Unlike Developing ELLs, who are still acquiring basic language skills and vocabulary, Long- Term ELLs require targeted interventions to address specific gaps, such as composing complex sentences or understanding grade-level academic texts. They should engage in rigorous content-area lessons with linguistic scaffolds to access grade-level material successfully. In accordance with Part 154 of New York State’s Education Law, schools would ensure that Long-Term ELLs receive instruction through appropriate language programs, such as English as a New Language (ENL) or bilingual education programs, tailored to their needs. Schools are also required to provide supplemental support services, including counseling, special education if necessary, and additional academic resources. Regular assessments should monitor both language and academic progress, with interventions adjusted based on those results. Families of Long-Term ELLs should be actively engaged in their children’s education, with communication provided in their home language to foster strong school-home partnerships. Following these guidelines ensures that Long-Term ELLs receive the support needed to achieve academic success and full proficiency in English and content-area knowledge. Many students demonstrate the ability to communicate orally with increasing complexity and accuracy, suggesting that instructional strategies emphasizing oral language development have been effective. Listening skills also show steady growth, although slightly lower than Speaking, reflecting the need for continued scaffolding in comprehension strategies. Reading and Writing remain the modalities of greatest need. For example, lower-grade students, particularly in grades 1 and 2, continue to struggle with foundational reading skills, comprehension, and writing conventions. Across the school, a significant number of students demonstrate difficulty producing written responses with sufficient academic vocabulary and grammatical accuracy. These trends highlight the importance of focused, modality-specific interventions, such as structured writing workshops, explicit vocabulary instruction, guided reading groups, and targeted comprehension strategies. Comparing NYSESLAT results with ELA 3-8 assessment data further underscores the correlation between lower performance in Reading and Writing on NYSESLAT and academic challenges in ELA. This alignment suggests the need for integrated instructional approaches where content-area learning reinforces language development. For instance, teachers are adapting lessons to include scaffolded reading tasks, modeled writing exercises, collaborative discussions, and frequent formative assessments to monitor progress and adjust instruction. Instruction is differentiated according to student proficiency levels, with additional stand-alone ENL sessions, small-group intervention, and scaffolded integrated instruction used to strengthen areas of need. What is the relationship between students’ performance on the NYSESLAT and other state assessments such as the ELA assessment, the Math assessment, and the Regents exams? Based on the Spring 2025 NYSESLAT results and the 2025 state assessments in ELA and Math at P.S. 7 Queens, several trends emerge regarding the relationship between English Language Proficiency (ELP) and academic performance. Students at higher NYSESLAT proficiency levels, such as Expanding and Transitioning, generally demonstrate stronger performance on both ELA and Math assessments. For example, in grade 3, students classified as Expanding on the NYSESLAT frequently scored at Level 3 or 4 on the ELA exam, indicating that higher language proficiency supports comprehension, writing, and engagement with grade-level content. Similarly, in Math, Expanding and Transitioning students showed greater success, particularly in solving word problems that require understanding of academic language. Former ELLs, especially those at the Commanding or Expanding levels, also tended to perform well across both subjects, demonstrating that continued language growth supports overall academic achievement. Students at the Entering and Emerging stages of NYSESLAT proficiency typically face more challenges. For instance, in grades 1 and 2, many students in these proficiency levels scored at Level 1 on ELA assessments, reflecting the dual challenges of developing English skills while mastering foundational literacy concepts. These students also exhibited lower performance in Math, highlighting the critical role of language in accessing and understanding mathematical content. Across grades 3–5, students with stronger Listening and Speaking skills on the NYSESLAT were more likely to perform well in ELA, underscoring the importance of oral language development as a foundation for reading comprehension and writing proficiency. The correlation between language proficiency and academic outcomes informs instructional planning at P.S. 7. Teachers integrate language and content goals by embedding academic vocabulary and language structures directly into ELA and Math lessons. For example, Math instruction includes explicit teaching of problem-solving terminology, sentence frames, and scaffolded word problems to support comprehension for ELLs at all proficiency levels. In ELA, lessons incorporate guided reading, modeled writing, and targeted vocabulary instruction aligned with NYSESLAT language objectives, ensuring that students are simultaneously developing English proficiency and grade-level literacy skills. These data trends underscore the need for differentiated instruction tailored to students’ proficiency levels. Students at Expanding and Transitioning levels benefit from integrated ENL instruction and scaffolded content lessons to deepen their academic language and conceptual understanding. Entering and Emerging students receive additional stand-alone ENL support, small-group interventions, and targeted literacy and numeracy instruction to build foundational skills and close gaps. Regular progress monitoring allows teachers to adjust instruction and ensure that all ELLs are making measurable growth in both language proficiency and academic achievement. In conclusion, the 2025 data reinforces the strong relationship between NYSESLAT performance and success on state ELA and Math assessments. As students’ English proficiency increases, their capacity to engage with grade- level content also improves, highlighting the importance of integrating language development into academic instruction and designing lessons that address both linguistic and content-area goals. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 184 of 283 9. What are trends reflected in the NYSESLAT results of ELL subgroups such as ELLs with IEPs and SIFE? What do you think is impacting the results for these subgroups? Based on the Spring 2025 NYSESLAT results and the P.S. 7 Queens Spring 2025 EDAT report, several trends emerge regarding English Language Proficiency (ELP) among ELL subgroups, particularly ELLs with IEPs and SIFE (Students with Interrupted Formal Education). These subgroups exhibit distinct challenges that highlight the need for targeted, differentiated instruction to support both language development and academic growth. ELLs with IEPs at P.S. 7 continue to demonstrate lower levels of proficiency on the NYSESLAT compared to their non-IEP peers. For example, in grade 3, of the 20 ELLs with IEPs assessed, 11 scored at the Entering level, 5 scored at the Emerging level, and only 4 reached the Expanding level. This pattern indicates that students with IEPs face compounded challenges, as they are learning English while also addressing their individualized learning goals. The 2025 EDAT report further indicates that ELLs with IEPs have higher absenteeism rates and may require additional support to engage consistently with instruction. To address these challenges, P.S. 7 has strengthened collaboration between ENL and special education staff, ensuring that lesson planning integrates language objectives with IEP goals. Targeted supports include small-group literacy interventions, scaffolded writing instruction, and differentiated content-based ENL lessons designed to meet both academic and language needs. SIFE students similarly show unique challenges in English language acquisition. Many SIFE students assessed in the Spring 2025 NYSESLAT scored predominantly at the Entering and Emerging levels, particularly in Writing and Speaking. For instance, in grade 4, of 22 SIFE students, 13 scored at Entering, 6 at Emerging, and only 3 at Expanding. These students often have interrupted formal education, which contributes to gaps in foundational literacy, numeracy, and academic knowledge. The EDAT report highlights that SIFE students experience higher rates of absenteeism and inconsistent engagement with instructional supports, which can further slow language proficiency growth. To address these needs, SIFE students at P.S. 7 receive targeted interventions, such as intensive literacy blocks, one-on-one or small-group language instruction, and scaffolded content-area lessons that use visuals, manipulatives, and sentence frames to reinforce both English development and academic understanding. Several factors contribute to the lower proficiency levels observed among ELLs with IEPs and SIFE students. Interrupted schooling often results in gaps in first-language literacy and numeracy, which can hinder English language acquisition. Additionally, absenteeism disrupts continuity in learning, limiting opportunities for consistent language exposure and skill practice. At P.S. 7, the school addresses these challenges through differentiated instruction, regular progress monitoring, and close collaboration between ENL and special education staff. Supports include individualized learning plans, targeted interventions in reading and writing, and embedded academic language instruction in math and content lessons. For SIFE students, instruction is designed to simultaneously build foundational literacy while accelerating language development, ensuring that they can access grade-level content more effectively. In conclusion, the 2025 NYSESLAT and EDAT data confirm that ELLs with IEPs and SIFE students at P.S. 7 face persistent challenges in achieving language proficiency. These trends highlight the critical importance of tailored instructional strategies, including scaffolded language support, targeted literacy interventions, collaboration with special education staff, and differentiated content-area instruction. By providing these specialized supports and regularly monitoring student progress, P.S. 7 works to ensure that all ELL subgroups can make meaningful growth in both English proficiency and academic achievement. 10. Tier 1 Core Instruction 1. How does your school provide ELLs with a high-quality academic experience that leads to deeper learning? At P.S. 7, we are committed to providing a high-quality, holistic academic experience for English Language Learners (ELLs), in alignment with CR Part 154. Our approach prioritizes intensive, proficiency-based English as a New Language (ENL) instruction tailored to individual language levels and academic needs. Students at the Entering and Emerging levels receive focused stand-alone ENL instruction to build foundational listening, speaking, reading, and writing skills, while Transitioning, Expanding, and Commanding students engage in integrated ENL within content- area instruction to reinforce academic language in real-world contexts. ELA instruction is delivered using the HMH Into Reading curriculum, and students developing Home Language Arts skills are supported through the Arriba la Lectura program, ensuring literacy development in both English and their home language. Our curriculum is culturally responsive, embedding diverse cultural perspectives and experiences into lessons. For example, in project-based learning units, students research community topics using bilingual resources, collaborate to present findings in both English and their home language, and create multimedia presentations that reflect their cultural backgrounds. Inquiry-based instruction is also incorporated, allowing students to explore questions connected to their experiences while practicing academic language and critical thinking skills. These approaches make learning meaningful and deepen engagement, helping students transfer language skills across content areas. Teachers receive ongoing professional development focused on culturally responsive practices and strategies to differentiate instruction based on language proficiency levels. Scaffolded supports—such as visuals, sentence frames, collaborative group work, and targeted academic vocabulary—are used to ensure that all students, regardless of their ENL level, can access grade-level content and meet academic expectations. Regular assessments inform individualized learning plans, enabling teachers to adjust instruction and provide additional interventions when needed. Beyond academics, we prioritize social-emotional support through counseling, mentoring, and peer networks, recognizing that a strong sense of belonging enhances motivation and learning outcomes. By integrating English language development with home language support and culturally relevant, inquiry-driven instruction, P.S. 7 fosters deeper learning, critical thinking, and academic success for ELLs across all program models, preparing them to thrive both within our school community and beyond. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 187 of 283 2. How does your school’s leadership team ensure ELLs have access to core instruction/curriculum? Our school's Instructional Leadership Team (ILT) is a vertical team with representatives from each grade level, as well as from our Dual Language, ENL, Special Education, and AIS Instructional Departments. This team collaborates closely to determine universal strategies that support and scaffold the learning of our Multilingual Learners (MLs) and English Language Learners (ELLs). To ensure high-quality support, continuity in ENL instruction, and targeted teacher development, a designated Assistant Principal oversees ENL programming and provides ongoing guidance. During ILT meetings, team members collaboratively develop and refine instructional practices specifically designed to meet the needs of MLs and ELLs. These strategies promote active engagement and accessibility across all content areas, ensuring lessons provide multiple entry points for students at varying language proficiency levels. Recommended strategies include the use of verbal and nonverbal cues, visual aids, manipulatives, hands-on activities, gestures, eye movements, and small-group instruction. Grade-level teams integrate these approaches into lesson planning to foster student independence while scaffolding learning opportunities to ensure meaningful access to the curriculum for MLs, ELLs, and Students with Disabilities (SWDs). The ILT monitors the effectiveness of these strategies by analyzing multiple sources of data, including classroom observations, formative and summative assessments, ENL proficiency measures, and subgroup-specific progress reports. Progress is regularly tracked for each student subgroup, and instructional adjustments are made based on student response and assessment outcomes. For example, if a group of ELLs demonstrates difficulty with reading comprehension or academic vocabulary, teachers may implement targeted small-group instruction, incorporate additional scaffolds, or adjust lesson pacing. The ILT, in collaboration with the ENL Assistant Principal, ensures that these data-driven adjustments are applied consistently across classrooms, maintaining inclusive and responsive instruction that meets the diverse needs of all students. 3. Describe how core content is delivered in each program model. Specify language, and the instructional approaches and methods to foster language development and meet the demands of the Next Generation Learning Standards. In our Dual Language and English as a New Language (ENL) programs, core content is delivered through differentiated instruction aligned with the Next Generation Learning Standards (NGLS) and designed to support both language development and academic achievement. Instruction is scaffolded to meet the needs of students across all proficiency levels—Entering, Emerging, Transitioning, Expanding, and Commanding—using techniques such as modeling, activating prior knowledge, total physical response, and connecting lessons to students’ real-life experiences to ensure accessibility for all learners. The Dual Language Program operates on a self-contained, alternating-day model in which instruction alternates between the student’s first language (L1) and the target language (L2). For example, Spanish-speaking students receive instruction in Spanish on one day and in English on the next. This approach allows students to develop academic skills in their dominant language and transfer them to the target language, supporting bilingual proficiency. Both English Language Learners (ELLs) and English Proficient (EP) students are integrated linguistically across content areas, with literacy instruction following a balanced, dual-language approach. Lessons integrate listening, speaking, reading, writing, observation, and critical thinking through meaningful activities in both languages. Teachers employ strategies such as visual aids, manipulatives, hands-on activities, authentic songs, literature, rhymes, and culturally relevant artifacts to strengthen comprehension and cultural understanding. Feedback techniques, including elicitation, repetition, metalinguistic clues, and nonverbal cues, are used to scaffold language development. Instruction is tailored to students’ developmental needs and proficiency levels in both languages, with new students paired with peers fluent in both L1 and L2 to foster language exchange and social integration. Side-by-side classroom teachers collaborate regularly, analyzing formative assessment data to co-plan lessons that clearly align language and content objectives with NGLS learning outcomes. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 188 of 283 The ENL Program, which includes both push-in and pull-out services, provides targeted support in English to help ELLs access core content while meeting NGLS standards. Lessons incorporate both language and content objectives, ensuring students develop academic knowledge alongside English language skills. Teachers activate prior knowledge and introduce vocabulary through adaptive texts, leveled study guides, trade books, graphic organizers, and thinking maps. Engagement and comprehension are supported through strategies such as jigsaw reading, note-taking outlines, hands-on materials, visuals, and realia. Instruction is scaffolded from teacher-led activities to interactive shared work, followed by independent or small-group tasks, with teacher conferences addressing individual needs. Structured language support, including vocabulary building, syntax exercises, and sentence modeling, is integrated to strengthen both academic understanding and English proficiency. Verbal and nonverbal cues, gestures, eye contact, and physical responses further enhance communication and engagement. Through these Dual Language and ENL program models, students engage in language-rich, culturally responsive learning experiences that support English proficiency or bilingual proficiency. Scaffolded instruction, aligned with NGLS, fosters academic growth, critical thinking, and independence for students across all language proficiency levels while ensuring equitable access to core content. 4. Simeone 190 of 283 Instructional strategies for supporting students with interrupted formal education (SIFE) are designed to bridge comprehension gaps, promote language development, and ensure access to grade-level content. These strategies include scaffolded language support using techniques such as Total Physical Response (TPR), gestures, body language, and visuals to connect vocabulary and concepts to meaning. Teachers provide explicit vocabulary instruction using realia, visual aids, and pictures, and pre-teach key terms related to upcoming lessons to prepare students for new content. Native language support is incorporated whenever possible, including bilingual dictionaries, side-by-side bilingual texts, and peer support from students who speak the same home language, promoting cross-linguistic transfer in both ENL and bilingual settings. Grade-level materials are selected and adapted to make content accessible for SIFE students. This includes leveled readers, adapted texts, and graphic organizers that simplify sentence structures while preserving core concepts. Hands-on materials—such as manipulatives in math, interactive diagrams in social studies, and visual models in science—help make abstract ideas concrete. Technology tools, including language apps, digital dictionaries, and educational platforms, provide multimodal support that enhances both language acquisition and subject-area knowledge. Instructional approaches are carefully structured to provide continuous support for language development. Teachers model tasks and language slowly and repetitively, breaking complex content into manageable parts and providing guided practice opportunities. Small-group instruction allows for targeted support and individualized attention, while peer collaboration in paired or group activities encourages language modeling, social interaction, and content comprehension. In bilingual settings, students are encouraged to transfer skills and vocabulary between their home language and English, strengthening both language proficiency and academic understanding. Ongoing assessment and progress monitoring are integral to informing SIFE instruction. Formative assessments— including observational check-ins, language development quizzes, reading and writing tasks, and oral reflections— allow teachers to identify gaps in comprehension and language use. These results are used to adjust instruction, reteach concepts, provide additional scaffolds, and design targeted interventions that meet students’ evolving needs. Cross-linguistic transfer is reinforced through consistent monitoring of how skills in the home language support English language development, particularly in literacy and content-area learning. Culturally responsive practices are embedded throughout instruction, valuing students’ unique cultural backgrounds and encouraging them to share personal experiences that connect to content. In compliance with CR Part 154, instruction is differentiated based on language proficiency and academic needs, with clear language and content objectives established for each student. These strategies are designed to support SIFE students in overcoming educational gaps, accessing grade-level content, and achieving both academic and English language proficiency, while fostering confidence, engagement, and deeper learning. c. Long Term ELLs At P.S. 7, instruction for Long-Term English Language Learners (LTELs) is intentionally designed to address the unique needs of students who have been in U.S. schools for five or more years but have not yet reached full English proficiency. Unlike Developing ELLs, LTELs receive targeted instruction that emphasizes academic language development, including complex sentence structures, specialized vocabulary, and formal writing skills. Teachers scaffold academic tasks using strategies such as sentence frames, structured note-taking, graphic organizers, and interactive media to support higher-order thinking and content mastery. Lessons are intentionally designed with both language and content objectives, integrating comprehension strategies such as close reading, think-alouds, and cooperative learning to help LTELs engage deeply with texts. Peer collaboration is also leveraged, pairing LTELs with proficient language peers to model academic language and provide support during group work. Grade-level materials for LTELs include leveled and adaptive texts, multimedia resources, digital vocabulary tools, and content-specific interactive platforms that allow students to practice English while accessing rigorous academic content. Instructional approaches are tiered based on students’ language proficiency, offering a continuum from guided practice to independent application. Writing instruction is a central focus, with explicit lessons on thesis development, paragraph structure, and argumentation to strengthen academic writing. Instruction also integrates culturally responsive practices, celebrating LTELs’ cultural backgrounds and fostering motivation, confidence, and a sense of belonging. Social-emotional supports, including mentoring, peer guidance, and goal-setting, are embedded within academic instruction to re-engage students and encourage persistence. Progress for LTELs is closely monitored through both formative and summative assessments, including observations, quizzes, projects, and writing tasks. Data from these assessments, along with review of prior academic work, informs targeted interventions and instructional adjustments, ensuring that achievement gaps are addressed and students continue to develop both English proficiency and content knowledge. These strategies are implemented in alignment with CR Part 154, ensuring LTELs receive differentiated, intensive language support while accessing the same curriculum as their peers, with instruction tailored to their linguistic, cognitive, and academic needs. 5. How is home language assessed in each program model (DLBE, TBE, and ENL)? In the Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE) program, home language assessment begins at enrollment and continues throughout a student’s participation in the program, tracking development in both the home language and English. Teachers assess language proficiency through standardized assessments and ongoing observations, evaluating the student’s ability to process and produce content in their native language. Family input is integral to this process, as parents and guardians provide insights into their child’s use of the home language at home, which helps educators understand the student’s linguistic environment. Collaborative planning among DLBE teachers ensures that assessment data is shared and instructional strategies are aligned across grades, promoting consistency in language support and helping track outcomes such as academic progress, language growth, and successful transfer of skills from the home language to English. In the Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) program, students’ home language proficiency is initially determined through home language surveys and language assessments in reading, writing, listening, and speaking. These assessments inform the level of home language support required. Students with lower proficiency in their home language may receive more substantial support in that language until English proficiency develops. Regular assessments and collaborative teacher check-ins monitor progress in both the home language and English, ensuring that instructional practices support the transfer of academic skills. Parent and community involvement provides additional context for home language development and ensures that instruction is culturally and linguistically responsive. Shared planning among TBE educators aligns strategies across classrooms, fostering instructional consistency and improving student outcomes. In the English as a New Language (ENL) program, home language assessments help determine the appropriate level of support a student needs, particularly during the early stages of language acquisition. Teachers use formal assessments and informal observations to evaluate students’ use of their home language in both social and academic contexts. Instruction is delivered through integrated or stand-alone ENL models, with collaborative planning among ENL, general education, and bilingual staff ensuring alignment of language objectives with content goals. Progress is regularly monitored in both the home language and English, allowing teachers to adjust instruction and ensure continued growth. Across all program models, CR Part 154 requirements guide the use of home language assessments to provide DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 193 of 283 accurate, comprehensive information that informs instruction. Collaboration and shared planning across teachers are tracked through ongoing progress monitoring, team meetings, and alignment of lesson objectives, ensuring consistent instructional approaches that support bilingualism, biliteracy, and academic success in both languages. 7. Which screening assessments (e.g.iReady, MAP Growth, Acadience, or STAR Reading) does your school use to guide instructional planning for your ELLs? Acadience and MAP Growth screening data are used to guide instructional planning for ELLs. Data from these assessments are disaggregated by language proficiency, allowing teachers to identify specific literacy needs in phonemic awareness, phonics, fluency, vocabulary, and comprehension. Based on this analysis, students receive tiered supports tailored to their skill levels, including small-group instruction, peer-assisted practice, and explicit teacher feedback. Progress is monitored continuously, and instructional groupings and strategies are adjusted over time to respond to students’ growth, ensuring that ELLs receive targeted support that promotes both language development and academic achievement. 12. For more intensive interventions, small-group instruction is offered through Rigby Guided Reading, Estrellita for Spanish literacy, and iChineseReader! for Chinese literacy. In Math, Envision Math is used with tiered instructional plans and manipulatives to address specific skill gaps. Simeone 197 of 283 Our school follows a data-driven approach within a Multi-Tiered System of Supports (MTSS) to guide instruction for English Language Learners (ELLs) at all grade levels. The Instructional Leadership Team (ILT) actively monitors school culture and climate to foster an environment of high expectations while prioritizing students’ emotional, physical, and psychological well-being, which are essential for optimal learning. Instruction is designed to address the multi-dimensional aspects of language—content, form, and use—ensuring that ELLs develop a well-rounded foundation in English. Literacy and language development are centered on key reading components, including phonemic awareness, phonics, fluency, vocabulary, and comprehension, in alignment with National Literacy Panel recommendations. Classroom activities incorporate reading, speaking, and writing tasks that focus on high-utility vocabulary, supporting consistent literacy protocols and facilitating language growth across all classrooms. Within the MTSS framework, tiered supports are determined by ongoing assessment data, including benchmark screeners and formative assessments. Tier 1 consists of universal supports such as culturally and linguistically responsive teaching, scaffolded instruction, and texts that reflect students’ home languages and cultural backgrounds. Tier 2 supports are implemented for students who require additional, targeted instruction, often delivered in small groups or through integrated lessons, and focus on reinforcing specific language or content skills. Tier 3 provides intensive, individualized interventions for students with the greatest instructional needs, including those with IEPs or students requiring accelerated English language development. Progress monitoring occurs regularly for all tiers, guiding instructional adjustments, regrouping, and the provision of additional scaffolds as needed to ensure students meet language and academic goals. In our bilingual programs, a shared curriculum across languages promotes strategic biliteracy development, cross- linguistic transfer, and metalinguistic awareness, all of which support greater language proficiency. Foundational literacy instruction in grades K–2 follows a structured daily scope and sequence that integrates sounds, spelling, and meaning through multi-sensory activities involving listening, speaking, reading, and writing. Interactive read- alouds and shared reading experiences expose students to grade-level and above-grade-level texts, fostering both language and content knowledge in context. Newly admitted students undergo a structured intake process to assess their instructional strengths and needs, supporting an inclusive and smooth transition. Teachers engage in ongoing professional development aligned with ELL instructional goals and receive coaching and feedback to refine their practice. Units and lessons are scaffolded and customized based on continuous assessment data, allowing teachers to focus on instructional delivery while applying specific supports tailored to students’ English proficiency levels and home languages. This comprehensive, data-informed approach ensures that all ELLs receive individualized supports and have equitable access to rigorous, standards-aligned learning across all content areas. FORMATIVE 14. Which formative assessments (e.g. NYC performance Tasks, ELL Periodic Assessment, HLA Assessment, DRA, teacher-created assessments, etc.) are used to inform and drive instruction? Our school employs a variety of formative assessments to guide instructional planning and differentiate instruction for English Language Learners (ELLs). These assessments include HMH Into Reading weekly and module assessments, Amira for reading fluency and comprehension, Fundations unit tests for word study, iReady for grades 3–5, MAP for grades K–5, and Acadience for grades K–2. In Spanish Dual Language classes, we use Estrellita for grades K–1, Senderos and Palabras a su Paso for grades 2–3, and Amira for reading fluency and comprehension. In Chinese Dual Language classes (grades K–1), teachers use teacher-created assessments tailored to Chinese language instruction. Formative data from these assessments is reviewed regularly to inform daily instructional decisions, allowing teachers to identify students’ strengths and areas of need. This information guides the differentiation of tasks, the grouping of students for small-group instruction, and the use of targeted scaffolds, DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 198 of 283 ensuring that ELLs at varying proficiency levels receive the appropriate level of support in reading, vocabulary, comprehension, and other language skills. Teachers adjust lesson pacing, provide additional practice or enrichment, and implement interventions based on ongoing data to promote consistent academic and language growth. 15. How do you ensure that MLs/ELLs are appropriately evaluated in their home languages throughout the year? To ensure that Multilingual Learners (MLs) and English Language Learners (ELLs) are effectively evaluated in their home languages throughout the year, we implement several targeted assessment practices: Spanish-speaking new admits complete the Spanish Lab assessment within the same 10-day period as the NYSITELL. This provides an initial measure of proficiency in their home language. ELLs have the option to take state math and science exams in their home language, ensuring content knowledge is assessed accurately and without language barriers. Teachers assess foundational literacy skills in Spanish using grade-appropriate resources, such as Estrellita for kindergarten and grade 1, and Senderos along with the word study program Palabras a Su Paso for grades 2 and 3. This data is submitted to and discussed with administration on a quarterly basis to monitor progress and tailor support. In Chinese Dual Language classes, teachers design authentic assessments to evaluate students' literacy and content understanding in Chinese, ensuring a culturally and linguistically responsive approach. Chinese Dual Language teachers also assist with the Home Language Identification Survey (HLIS) interviews at the beginning of the year, supporting accurate and comprehensive identification of students' language backgrounds. What is your NYSESLAT administration plan? How does your school ensure that all ELLs are tested with the NYSESLAT annually? Include the titles of the staff that will administer and score the assessment. Please describe your plan to ensure that all ELLs and former ELLs continue to receive mandated instruction during the testing period. Our NYSESLAT administration plan aligns with the NYCDOE Multilingual Learners guidelines and CR Part 154 regulations, ensuring that all ELLs and former ELLs continue receiving mandated instruction during the testing period. The assessment is administered by a team of trained educators, including ENL teachers, bilingual teachers, and other qualified staff members who have undergone specific training in assessment procedures. This team, which also includes assistant principals, oversees the process to ensure both accuracy and compliance. Scoring is led by certified ENL and bilingual teachers who collaborate to ensure consistency and accuracy in scoring that meets NYSESLAT standards. These scoring teams are provided with the necessary time, materials, and support for effective calibration and collaboration. To maintain continuity of instruction and minimize disruption, we implement a rotating testing schedule. This schedule is designed to limit the impact on core instruction for ELLs and former ELLs. Where possible, we use dedicated spaces and testing windows to avoid removing students from their regularly scheduled classes. Non- testing ELLs and former ELLs continue to receive their mandated instructional services without interruption. For ELLs and former ELLs who are not participating in testing, classrooms continue to operate with scheduled instructional activities, focusing on content-area work and language development. Teachers collaborate to adapt lesson plans during this period to meet the needs of ELLs and ensure instructional goals remain aligned with language and content standards. This plan prioritizes both compliance with assessment requirements and the continuous academic progress of all multilingual learners in our school community. 18. How does your school ensure that current and former ELLs receive necessary accommodations for state assessments, including the Specialized High School Admissions Test (SHSAT) if applicable? Our school ensures that current and former ELLs receive the necessary accommodations for state assessments in accordance with CR Part 154 regulations and state mandates for ELLs. We adhere to several key practices to ensure equitable access to assessments. First, we identify appropriate accommodations for ELLs based on their individual language proficiency levels and specific needs. This process involves reviewing student IEPs, language needs, and any prior accommodation requests to ensure all eligible students receive the necessary supports during state assessments. For students currently identified as ELLs, accommodations include extended time, bilingual glossaries, translation of test directions, and access to bilingual or nonverbal test formats as required. These accommodations are DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 200 of 283 documented in students’ Individualized Education Programs (IEPs) or 504 Plans and are implemented for all state assessments. Former ELLs, as defined by CR Part 154, are eligible for specific accommodations for up to two years after exiting ELL status. For these students, we ensure accommodations such as extra time and clarification of test instructions are provided during assessments to promote equitable testing conditions. To support the successful implementation of accommodations, we provide training for teachers and administrators on the specific supports available for ELLs. Staff are made aware of their responsibilities in implementing these accommodations, and we coordinate with assessment teams to ensure consistency across all testing situations. Additionally, we regularly monitor the accommodations process throughout the year. Feedback is gathered from teachers, families, and students to assess the effectiveness of the accommodations. Based on this feedback, we make adjustments as needed to ensure compliance and fairness during testing. This comprehensive approach guarantees that current and former ELLs receive the accommodations necessary to succeed, in full compliance with state mandates and Part 154 regulations. Annual Individual Meeting to discuss goals of the program, language development progress, language proficiency assessment results, and language development needs in all content areas. Note: this meeting is separate from and does not include the mandated ELL program orientation meeting and DOE-scheduled parent-teacher conferences. Our school ensures that families of English Language Learners (ELLs) are provided with the required annual individual meetings in compliance with CR Part 154. These meetings are scheduled with flexibility to maximize family participation, offering morning, afternoon, and evening time slots on different days of the week to accommodate varying work schedules and family commitments. Additionally, virtual meeting options are available via platforms such as Zoom or Teams for parents who cannot attend in person. Families are notified in advance through multiple channels, including phone calls, emails, and written notices in their home language, ensuring they are aware of the meeting details and expectations. During the annual individual meetings, school staff discuss the goals of the ELL program, the student’s language development progress, results from English language proficiency assessments (including NYSESLAT and classroom-based measures), and language development needs across all content areas. Teachers provide specific examples of the student’s academic performance, highlight strengths, and identify areas where targeted support is needed. Families are encouraged to ask questions, share insights about their child’s home language use, and collaborate in developing individualized strategies to support continued language and academic growth. If a family is unable to attend the scheduled meeting, follow-up procedures are in place. School staff provide a summary of the meeting in the family’s home language and offer opportunities to schedule a make-up meeting or a one-on-one virtual session. Families are supported with translated documents, summaries, and individualized guidance to ensure they fully understand their child’s progress and next steps. Detailed records, including attendance logs, meeting agendas, discussion summaries, and action plans, are maintained in the student information system, ensuring accountability and accessibility for authorized personnel. Importantly, the information and input gathered from these meetings directly inform instructional planning and targeted interventions for ELL students. Teachers and program specialists use family insights and assessment data to adjust lesson scaffolds, groupings, and language supports, ensuring instruction is aligned with each student’s strengths, needs, and goals. Through these practices, our school not only meets CR Part 154 requirements but also fosters a collaborative and inclusive environment where families are empowered to actively participate in their child’s education, reinforcing communication between home and school and supporting the academic and language development of ELL students. Ensures families receive all school communications and documents in the language they best understand. How are families able to respond or initiate communications in languages other than English? Our school ensures that families of English Language Learners (ELLs) receive all communications in their preferred language and can respond or initiate communications in languages other than English, in alignment with the NYCDOE Multilingual Learners manual. We utilize an online communication platform, ClassDojo, which translates announcements, newsletters, and important documents into multiple languages spoken by our student community. Families can select their preferred language settings within the platform, ensuring they receive information in a format they understand, promoting inclusivity and equitable access to school communications. The platform also supports multilingual messaging, allowing parents to respond to communications, ask questions, and engage in dialogue with teachers and school staff in their preferred language. This ensures that language barriers do not impede parents’ ability to stay informed, participate actively in their child’s education, or provide feedback. To support families in using the platform, our school provides guidance through tutorials, step-by-step instructions, and one-on-one assistance when needed. Bilingual staff are available to help parents navigate the platform, troubleshoot technical issues, and ensure they are comfortable accessing information and communicating effectively. Additionally, staff receive ongoing training on using translation tools and resources integrated into the platform, ensuring communications are accurate and that families’ preferred languages are consistently supported. By leveraging technology and providing personalized support, our school strengthens parent-school partnerships, enhances accessibility, and ensures that all families, regardless of their primary language, are fully engaged in their child’s education and school community. b. Insights are gathered from families, staff, and community partners through monthly Principal and Student Town Hall Meetings to identify areas where support is most needed. Based on these findings, we offer culturally relevant workshops on topics such as navigating the education system, supporting academic success at home, and advocating for children’s educational needs. Leadership programs provide parents with skills to actively participate in school governance and advocacy, while cultural celebrations and community events promote cross-cultural understanding and inclusivity. To further support engagement, we organize digital literacy training sessions, helping families effectively use educational technology, online platforms, and communication tools, enabling them to participate fully in their child’s learning journey. Participation in all activities is carefully documented, including attendance records, workshop materials, and survey responses, in accordance with CR Part 154 guidelines. Feedback is collected through surveys, focus groups, and informal discussions, and this data is stored securely to track family engagement and inform future planning. Insights from participation and feedback are used to evaluate the impact of initiatives, adapt programs, and expand offerings to better meet the evolving needs of families. Simeone 207 of 283 Part VIII: ELL Identification Attestation and Principal Certification Part VIII: ELL Identification Attestation and Principal Certification Identification Plan For additional information, refer to ELL Identification Attestation Professional Learning Module. 1. What are the titles of the members of your ELL identification team? Note: Members of the ELL identification team must be pedagogues. The members of our ELL identification team include an ENL Teacher, a Bilingual Teacher, an Assistant Principal, a Guidance Counselor, and a Special Education Teacher. Each member brings specialized expertise to the identification process. The ENL Teacher assesses students’ English language proficiency and provides insight into appropriate instructional placements. The Bilingual Teacher evaluates students’ home language abilities to determine language dominance and support needs. The Assistant Principal oversees the process, ensuring adherence to district and CR Part 154 guidelines and facilitating coordination among team members. The Guidance Counselor gathers and reviews academic and social-emotional information to provide a holistic understanding of each student’s learning profile. The Special Education Teacher identifies students’ potential learning differences and ensures that any needs related to disabilities are considered during placement decisions. Together, this multidisciplinary team ensures that each student is accurately assessed and placed in an ELL program that meets both their language development and academic needs. 2. Describe the structures and process in place for identification of ELLs year-round as required by the NYC DOE Policy & Reference Guide for MLs/ELLs, including your school’s identification plan for any incoming students with an Individualized Education Plan (IEP). Include: How your school proactively plans for the ELL identification process so that it does not interrupt the provision of mandated ENL and/or bilingual instruction to current, former and potential ELLs, Who is responsible for each step of the ELL identification process, and School’s plan for ensuring ELLs and former ELLs will continue to receive mandated instruction. At our school, we implement a comprehensive, year-round process for identifying English Language Learners (ELLs), in accordance with the NYC DOE Policy & Reference Guide for Multilingual Learners (MLs)/ELLs and CR Part 154 regulations. Identification begins at enrollment with a review of the Home Language Identification Survey (HLIS) for all incoming students. Families’ responses on the HLIS help determine if a language proficiency assessment is necessary. For example, when a new student, Mary, enrolled last year, her HLIS indicated Spanish was spoken at home, triggering a referral for assessment. Our ELL Identification Team—which includes the ENL Teacher, Bilingual Teacher, Assistant Principal, Guidance Counselor, and Special Education Teacher—reviews HLIS responses, evaluates student records, and coordinates next steps. Each team member contributes their expertise: ENL and Bilingual Teachers administer language proficiency assessments in English and the home language, the Assistant DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 208 of 283 Principal ensures compliance with CR Part 154 and coordinates scheduling, the Guidance Counselor provides academic and social-emotional context, and the Special Education Teacher ensures students with IEPs receive appropriate accommodations during testing. To minimize disruptions to mandated ENL or bilingual instruction, our school uses a rotating assessment schedule, testing new students during times that do not conflict with core instruction. For instance, during NYSESLAT or other proficiency testing windows, current ELLs continue receiving language services in dedicated classrooms, while incoming students are assessed in separate spaces. Students with IEPs receive accommodations such as extended time, oral assessments, or individualized support during testing, coordinated by ENL and Special Education staff. Once assessments are complete, the Identification Team reviews results, determines ELL eligibility, and documents each step, including assessment outcomes, team deliberations, and placement decisions, in the school’s student information system to ensure compliance, continuity, and accountability. Families are promptly notified of their child’s ELL status, program options, and available language supports, and any missed meetings are followed up with rescheduling opportunities or written summaries. Former ELLs are monitored for up to two years post-exit to ensure ongoing academic and language support. This structured approach ensures accurate identification, uninterrupted access to mandated instruction, and a seamless transition into appropriate ELL or bilingual programs for all students.
