Simeone 209 of 283 Principal Certification In accordance with New York State’s Commissioner’s Regulations Part 154 as outlined and implemented in the NYC DOE Policy and Reference Guide for MLs/ELLs, I, attest that the following ELL identification procedures and activities are adhered to, and staff are trained on the items listed below. The ELL Identification Process is completed for each student within 10 school days of enrollment (20 school days for students entering with IEPs). Enrollment status of each newly admitted student is determined: a. If a student has been in New York State public schools within the past 2 years, our school contacts the previous school to obtain ELL status, relevant assessment scores, and English language proficiency level. b. If a student has been outside of New York State public schools for more than 2 years, our school administers the ELL Identification Process as part of reentry. The home language of the student is determined by a trained and licensed pedagogue. a. The parent completes the Home Language Identification Survey in the parent’s preferred language; translation services are provided by a bilingual staff member, the DOE’s over-the-phone translation services, or an alternative, high quality translation service. An interview with parents and students in English and the home language is conducted. This includes reviewing documents, prior assessments, and academic experience. An assessment of the student’s abilities and/or work samples determines the student’s literacy and math level in his/her home language. Eligibility for the NYSITELL is determined. a. If the newly enrolled student is entering with an IEP, the school’s Language Proficiency Team (LPT) recommends eligibility to the principal, who obtains final approval from the superintendent. Students are administered the NYSITELL, if eligible. Notification letters are sent to the parent/guardian in their preferred language. a. Parent is notified of their child’s ELL status and results of the NYSITELL. If the student is identified as an ELL and the student’s home language is Spanish, the student is administered the Spanish Language Assessment Battery (LAB). Student with Interrupted/Inconsistent Formal Education (SIFE) status is determined using New York State’s resources. If a student is a newly identified ELL, the parent is invited to the parent orientation meeting. a. The parent orientation meeting provides parents/guardians with an explanation of each ELL program model offered in NYC; the parent video describing the three programs is presented (in parent’s preferred language); parents/guardians are encouraged to ask questions so that they are DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 210 of 283 informed about all ELL programs; translation and interpretation services are provided throughout the meeting. ELL is placed in the ELL program that the parent selected. a. If the bilingual program that the parent selected is not available, the parent is offered a transfer to a school with that bilingual program in accordance with NYC DOE transfer policies. b. If the ELL remains in the school, the ELL is placed in English as a New Language and our school keeps track of parent selection so that when minimum thresholds are met, we can open the ELL program of choice. Parents are notified that they have 45 days to appeal the ELL status of their child (ELL Re-identification Process). If the ELL Re-identification Process is implemented for any student, the principal reviews the decision of any student whose ELL status was changed because of the ELL Re-identification Process within 6 to 12 months. Copies of documentation regarding ELL identification, including letters and parent notifications are kept in the students’ cumulative files. Simeone 212 of 283 LTI Overview and Part A: Designation of a LAC LTI Overview and Part A: Designation of a LAC Chancellor’s Regulation A-663 establishes procedures for ensuring that Limited-English-Proficient (LEP) parents of students enrolled in the New York City public schools are provide with a meaningful opportunity to participate in and have access to programs and services critical to their child’s education. To this end, schools must review and complete the annual LTI Plan to address its language assistance needs consistent with the requirements of this regulation. The LTI Plan requires information on the following: Name of person designated as the Language Access Coordinator (LAC). Identification and assessment of LEP parent population. Identification of Critical Communications and standing, in-person meetings throughout the school year that require language assistance services and how the school plans to respond to these needs. Plan to ensure staff awareness of language access requirements and available resources. Provision of notice to parents on the available language assistance services. Methods for the school to solicit and implement feedback on its provision of language assistance services. Simeone 216 of 283 Part C: Parent Communications and Engagement Part C: Parent Communications and Engagement Parent Communication and Engagement Identification of written critical communications and in-person meetings throughout the school year that require language assistance services and how the school plans to respond to these needs. 1. List the documents your school typically distributes each year that require translation, along with your plan for translating these documents. Document How do you plan to implement the translation process? Please include procedures and resources to Type (e.g. ensure the timely delivery of translated documents to parents. parent flyers, IEPs, etc.) Parent To ensure that all parents receive important information in a language they can understand, our school Flyers has developed a clear process for translating documents and communicating with families. This process is designed to be efficient, timely, and inclusive, utilizing both digital and in-person resources. At the beginning of the school year, we collect language preference information from families through the Home Language Questionnaire (HLQ) during student registration. This helps us identify the languages spoken at home and ensures we know which families may need translated materials. For example, if a family indicates that Spanish is spoken at home, we ensure that all relevant school communications, such as flyers, newsletters, and event announcements, are translated into Spanish. Once the need for translation is identified, our school uses a combination of in-house staff and external professional translation services to ensure accuracy and cultural relevance. Our ENL specialists and bilingual teachers assist in translating documents into the languages spoken by our families, such as Spanish, Chinese, and Bengali. For more complex documents or those requiring specialized terminology (like medical forms), we partner with a professional translation service. This approach allows us to ensure that translations are both accurate and culturally appropriate. To ensure that translated materials are delivered to parents in a timely manner, our school has established a system for prompt distribution. For instance, when a flyer for an upcoming event, such as a parent workshop or cultural celebration, is prepared, it is sent for translation at least two weeks in advance. Once the translation is complete, the flyer is printed and distributed through multiple channels, including sending paper copies home with students, emailing the translated materials to families, and posting them on our school’s website and social media platforms. Additionally, if our school uses a communication app like Remind or ClassDojo, translated materials are uploaded to the app so that parents can access them at their convenience. In addition to translated flyers, we also provide in-person support for families who may need assistance understanding school documents. Our bilingual staff members, including ENL teachers, bilingual counselors, and parent coordinators, are available to meet with parents and explain important documents or school updates in their native language. We also organize language- DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 217 of 283 specific parent workshops where families can ask questions and receive additional support. To ensure that the translation process remains effective, we regularly seek feedback from parents about the clarity and usefulness of the translated materials. After major events, we might send out brief surveys asking if the translated information was clear and if there were any language barriers. This feedback is used to improve the quality and timeliness of future translations. Through this process, we aim to make sure that all parents are well-informed and able to actively participate in their children’s education, regardless of their language background, fostering family engagement and equitable access to educational opportunities for all students. Meeting How do you plan to offer interpretation services? Please outline the procedures and resources in Type (e.g. place to ensure timely access to interpretation for parents. parent workshops, PTCs) Parent Town For Parent Town Hall meetings hosted by the Principal, we are committed to ensuring that all parents Hall have access to accurate and timely interpretation services so they can fully participate in the discussions. To achieve this, we have established a well-organized process that relies on both our bilingual staff and available resources to ensure seamless language support during these important events. To begin, we identify the language needs of our families through the Home Language Questionnaire (HLQ) collected during student registration. This allows us to understand which languages are spoken at home and helps us anticipate the need for interpretation services. For instance, if a family indicates that they speak Spanish or Bengali, we plan for interpretation services in these languages during the Parent Town Hall meetings. For the Parent Town Hall meetings themselves, we utilize our bilingual school staff, including ENL teachers, counselors, and other support staff, to provide interpretation services. These staff members are trained and available to interpret real-time discussions and presentations in languages such as Spanish, Mandarin, or others commonly spoken by our families. This internal resource allows us to ensure that parents can follow the meeting and participate in the conversation. If the language demand exceeds the capacity of our staff, we also coordinate with external services to secure professional interpreters for less commonly spoken languages, ensuring no parent is left without support. To ensure timely access to interpretation services, we follow a clear set of procedures. Once the date for the Parent Town Hall meeting is confirmed, invitations and reminders are sent to parents in multiple languages. These communications provide meeting details, such as the agenda and the languages for which interpretation services will be available. We also include a contact number where parents can request additional language support or specific accommodations, like ASL interpretation, in case there are any last-minute needs. On the day of the meeting, our bilingual staff members are provided with the agenda and any presentation materials ahead of time to ensure they are prepared to interpret effectively. For larger meetings, we use microphones or headsets to facilitate simultaneous interpretation, ensuring that parents can follow along without interruptions. Additionally, we provide translated copies of presentation slides and any key materials before the meeting begins, allowing parents to review them in advance. During the meeting, bilingual staff assist parents with any questions or concerns, offering one-on-one translation as needed to ensure full participation. After the Parent Town Hall meeting, we seek feedback from parents about the effectiveness of the interpretation services. This feedback helps us continuously improve our process and better meet the needs of our diverse community. By relying on our bilingual staff and external services when DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 219 of 283 necessary, we ensure that all parents have equal access to the information shared during the Parent Town Hall meetings. This process strengthens family involvement and supports our commitment to creating an inclusive, equitable school environment where all families can engage in their children's education. Student For Student Town Hall meetings hosted by the Principal, we are dedicated to ensuring that all parents Town Hall have access to accurate and timely interpretation services to support their full participation in the event. To accomplish this, we have developed a clear, structured process that involves both professional resources and the assistance of bilingual staff to ensure all parents can engage meaningfully in the meeting. Our first step in providing interpretation services is to identify the language needs of our families. We do this by collecting language preference information through the Home Language Questionnaire (HLQ) during student registration. This allows us to know in advance which languages are spoken in our community and anticipate the need for interpretation during events like the Student Town Hall meetings. For example, if a family reports Spanish or Bengali as their primary language, we ensure that interpretation services for these languages are available during the meeting. For the Student Town Hall meeting itself, we utilize a combination of bilingual staff and, when necessary, professional interpreters. Our bilingual school staff, including ENL teachers, counselors, and other support personnel, are trained and available to assist with real- time interpretation for parents who speak languages such as Spanish, Mandarin, or other commonly spoken languages within our community. In the event that a language is less commonly spoken, we work with professional translation services to ensure that an interpreter is present for that language as well. This approach guarantees that parents from all linguistic backgrounds can understand the information shared during the meeting. The procedures for ensuring timely access to interpretation services are as follows: Once the date for the Student Town Hall meeting is set, invitations and reminders are sent out to parents in multiple languages, providing details about the meeting, its agenda, and the languages for which interpretation will be available. We also include a contact number for parents to request additional language support or any special accommodations, such as ASL interpretation, if needed. This allows us to make any necessary arrangements well in advance and ensures that we are fully prepared for the meeting. On the day of the meeting, our bilingual staff members are briefed and provided with any presentation materials ahead of time to ensure they are well-prepared for interpretation. For larger gatherings, we utilize microphones or headsets for simultaneous interpretation, ensuring that the process runs smoothly and parents can follow the conversation in real-time. Additionally, we make sure that translated copies of any presentation slides, handouts, or key materials are available before the meeting, so that parents can review them in advance. During the meeting, bilingual staff are stationed throughout the room to assist parents with any specific questions or concerns that may arise, and they offer one-on-one translation when needed. After the meeting, we gather feedback from parents regarding the quality and effectiveness of the interpretation services. This feedback helps us continuously improve our processes and better meet the needs of all families in the future. By utilizing both bilingual staff and professional interpreters, we ensure that all parents have equal access to the information shared during the Student Town Hall meetings, allowing them to stay informed and involved in their children's education. This process fosters a stronger connection between the school and families and supports our commitment to creating an inclusive and equitable school community. Describe the resources and processes your school will use to communicate with parents who speak a language other than English during an emergency (e.g., lockdown, fire, student attendance, nurse/hospital visit, etc.), or when a parent needs to contact the school in an emergency, ensuring communication is in their preferred language. In the event of an emergency, our school is dedicated to ensuring that all parents, including those who speak a language other than English, receive clear and timely communication in their preferred language. To achieve this, we utilize a combination of bilingual staff interpretation, translated messages via ClassDojo, translated robo-calls, and translated flyers to ensure that language barriers do not impede access to vital information. Our bilingual staff members, including ENL teachers, school counselors, and other support staff, play a crucial role in providing real-time interpretation during emergencies. If a parent needs to contact the school or requires assistance, they can reach out to one of our bilingual staff members, who are fluent in languages such as Spanish, Bengali, Mandarin, and others commonly spoken within our community. These staff members are trained to assist with interpretation quickly and effectively, ensuring that parents understand the emergency situation and any immediate actions they need to take. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 223 of 283 In addition to staff interpretation, we use ClassDojo, a communication platform that allows us to send messages in parents’ preferred languages. ClassDojo automatically translates messages into the language parents have indicated during registration, ensuring that all families receive important updates without delay. This is particularly useful for communicating during emergencies such as lockdowns, fire drills, or medical incidents, as it provides immediate, accessible information. For further outreach, we also utilize translated robo-calls. Our automated calling system sends emergency messages in multiple languages, including Spanish, Bengali, and Mandarin, ensuring that parents receive urgent information in their preferred language. These robo-calls are designed to deliver clear, actionable instructions during critical situations like school evacuations or health-related emergencies. Lastly, we distribute translated flyers and written notices to ensure all parents are kept informed. These materials are available in the main office and are sent home with students when necessary. In addition, any emergency-related forms or instructions are translated to ensure that parents have all the information they need in a language they understand. By leveraging the expertise of our bilingual staff, technology tools like ClassDojo and robo-calls, and translated printed materials, we ensure that all parents are able to receive timely and accurate information during emergencies. This multi-pronged approach not only ensures the safety and well-being of our students but also supports our ongoing commitment to creating an inclusive and equitable school environment where all families are empowered to respond to emergencies effectively. Simeone 224 of 283 Part D: Staff Awareness Part D: Staff Awareness Staff Awareness Plan to ensure staff awareness of language access requirements and available resources. 1. Describe how your school will ensure that all staff members complete the required asynchronous NYCPS Language Access Training for School-based Staff on the WeLearnNYC learning platform, and are familiar with the Best Practices for When to Provide Translation of Critical Written Communications to Parents and Best Practices for When to Provide Interpretation for Critical Oral Communications with Parents. Additionally, explain how your Galaxy-designated LAC will disseminate future language access resources and updates to all staff members. At P.S. 7Q, we are committed to ensuring that all staff members complete the required asynchronous NYCPS Language Access Training for School-based Staff on the WeLearnNYC learning platform and are familiar with the best practices for providing translation and interpretation services to parents. We have established clear procedures and ongoing support to ensure that staff are well-equipped to meet the language access needs of our school community. To ensure that all staff members complete the training, we have incorporated it into our professional development schedule. Staff members will receive reminders about the training via email and during staff meetings, where the importance of language access and the specific requirements of the training will be emphasized. Staff members will be given dedicated time to complete the training on WeLearnNYC, and we will monitor completion to ensure full participation. For staff who may need additional support, the assistant principal and ENL team will offer assistance and guidance on how to navigate the WeLearnNYC platform and complete the training. In addition to the training, our staff is provided with clear guidelines on when to provide translation for critical written communications and when to provide interpretation for critical oral communications with parents. These guidelines are informed by the NYC Department of Education's language access policies and include specific examples, such as providing translations for documents related to student enrollment, academic progress, emergency notifications, and consent forms. For oral communications, staff are trained to offer interpretation during parent-teacher conferences, emergency calls, and other important meetings where parents must receive clear and accurate information in their preferred language. To support the dissemination of language access resources and updates, our Galaxy-designated Language Access Coordinator (LAC) will take an active role in keeping all staff informed. The LAC will regularly communicate updates through staff meetings, emails, and the school’s internal communication platforms. Additionally, the LAC will coordinate the distribution of any new resources or training materials related to language access and will serve as a point of contact for staff who have questions or need further assistance. The LAC will also collaborate with the ENL team and other staff to ensure that the school remains compliant with language access policies and that all language access services are provided effectively and equitably. By ensuring that all staff members complete the required training, understand best practices for language access, DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. We also highlight language assistance services during various parent engagement events. For example, at events such as our PTA meetings, international parents' nights, and Open House, we ensure that parents are informed about the availability of language support services. During these events, we provide information about how to access language assistance and encourage parents to ask questions or express any specific language needs they may have. These events also offer an opportunity to directly engage with bilingual staff members who can provide more information and assist with any language barriers. ClassDojo automatically translates messages into the language parents select DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 227 of 283 during registration, ensuring they are kept informed about important school events, announcements, and deadlines. Lastly, we distribute the "Language Access for NYC Public Schools Families" brochure, which provides comprehensive information about the language assistance services offered by the school district. This brochure is shared during parent orientation, at school events, and is available in multiple languages to ensure parents are fully aware of their rights to language access services. By utilizing these diverse communication methods and resources, P.S. 7Q ensures that all families, regardless of their language, are informed about the language assistance services available to them and feel empowered to access the support they need. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 228 of 283 Part F: Monitoring Part F: Monitoring Monitoring Methods for soliciting and implementing feedback on language assistance services. 1. In addition to the feedback received from parents via OLA’s Translation & Interpretation Feedback Survey, describe the methods your school will use to gather feedback from parents regarding the language assistance services they receive in their preferred language. At P.S. 7Q, we are committed to ensuring that all parents are informed about the language assistance services available to them in their preferred language. To achieve this, we utilize a variety of resources and communication methods to ensure families are aware of the support they can access. One of the primary ways we inform parents is through translated signage displayed throughout the school. These signs are strategically placed in areas such as the main entrance, the school office, and other high-traffic areas, and they clearly indicate the availability of language assistance services. The signage is translated into the most commonly spoken languages among our parent population, such as Spanish, Bengali, and Chinese. In addition to signage, we distribute translated brochures, flyers, and letters to parents, which provide detailed information about available language assistance services. These documents are sent home with students, shared at parent engagement events, and made available in the main office. They outline how parents can access interpretation and translation services for meetings, documents, and communications. For example, the letters and flyers will explain how parents can request an interpreter for parent-teacher conferences, or how to request translated versions of critical school documents, such as consent forms or report cards. We also highlight language assistance services during various parent engagement events. For example, at events such as our PTA meetings, international parents' nights, and Open House, we ensure that parents are informed about the availability of language support services. During these events, we provide information about how to access language assistance and encourage parents to ask questions or express any specific language needs they may have. These events also offer an opportunity to directly engage with bilingual staff members who can provide more information and assist with any language barriers. To further support communication, we use messaging applications like ClassDojo to send translated messages to parents in their preferred language. ClassDojo automatically translates messages into the language parents select during registration, ensuring they are kept informed about important school events, announcements, and deadlines. Lastly, we distribute the "Language Access for NYC Public Schools Families" brochure, which provides comprehensive information about the language assistance services offered by the school district. This brochure is shared during parent orientation, at school events, and is available in multiple languages to ensure parents are fully aware of their rights to language access services. By utilizing these diverse communication methods and resources, P.S. 7Q ensures that all families, regardless of DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 229 of 283 their language, are informed about the language assistance services available to them and feel empowered to access the support they need. 2. How has your school worked to improve its language assistance services? Share any feedback results and outline your plans for further improvements in the upcoming school year. At P.S. 7Q, we are committed to continually improving our language assistance services to ensure that all families, regardless of their preferred language, have access to the support they need. We have dedicated resources to enhance accessibility by using data from our Home Language Survey and leveraging parent volunteers to better meet the diverse needs of our community. One key strategy we have implemented is the careful analysis of responses from the Home Language Survey, which helps us identify the languages spoken by our families. This survey provides valuable data that guides our decision- making process, allowing us to prioritize language services for the most common languages spoken by our parents, such as Spanish, Bengali, and Chinese. We ensure that our language support services, including translation and interpretation, are available in these languages and adapt as needed based on survey responses. Additionally, we actively engage parent volunteers who speak a variety of languages to assist with translation and interpretation during school events, parent-teacher conferences, and in other communications with families. These volunteers play a vital role in bridging language gaps and ensuring that all parents can participate fully in their child’s education. By incorporating parent volunteers into our language assistance efforts, we not only enhance communication but also build a stronger sense of community and collaboration. To gather feedback on our language assistance services, we regularly survey parents during events and through follow-up communications. This feedback has been invaluable in identifying areas for improvement. For example, parents have shared that they would like more translated materials for school-wide announcements and a clearer understanding of how to request interpretation services. In response to this feedback, we are working to provide more frequent updates and easily accessible instructions for parents on how to access interpretation services for school meetings and events. Looking ahead to the upcoming school year, we plan to further enhance our language assistance services by expanding the availability of translated documents, increasing the number of bilingual staff and volunteers, and hosting additional parent workshops to provide training on how to effectively navigate language access resources. We also plan to continue gathering parent feedback to ensure that our services evolve with the changing needs of our community. By focusing on data-driven decisions from the Home Language Survey, engaging parent volunteers, and listening to feedback, P.S. 7Q will continue to improve its language assistance services and ensure that all families feel supported and included in their children’s educational journey. The Title III supplemental instructional services must be based on student need. These supplemental services should complement core bilingual and ENL services required under CR Part 154. Direct supplemental services should be provided for before school, after school, and Saturday programs. Teachers providing the services must be bilingual education and/or English for Speakers of Other Languages (ESOL) certified teachers. High quality professional development that is "of sufficient intensity and duration to have a positive and lasting impact on the teachers’ performance in classrooms." Professional development activities should be well-planned, ongoing events rather than one-day or short-term workshops and conferences. Parent engagement and supports must ensure that there are appropriate translation and interpretation services to meet community needs. These are in addition to mandated activities, such as parent orientation during ELL identification process or annual ELL parent meetings. P.S. 7 recognizes the ongoing impact that recent disruptions have had on student learning and well-being, particularly among English Language Learners (ELLs). To address these needs, the school is implementing a comprehensive Academic Recovery and ENL Support Program designed to accelerate literacy and language development across grades K–5. The program directly serves the school’s current multilingual population, which includes 397 ELLs: 341 Newcomers, 56 Developing ELLs, and 0 Long-Term ELLs. ELL enrollment by grade level includes 63 kindergarten students, 64 first graders, 82 second graders, 82 third graders, 63 fourth graders, and 43 fifth graders. Instruction for Entering and Emerging ELLs will focus on foundational English vocabulary, reading accuracy and fluency, and the development of simple and compound oral and written sentence structures. Students at the Transitioning and Commanding levels, along with newly arrived non-ELLs needing similar support, will work on strengthening reading fluency, accuracy, and comprehension while expanding academic vocabulary and increasing their ability to use complex language structures aligned with grade-level expectations. Students enrolled in the Dual Language program will participate in the Dual Language After-School Academy, where they will receive targeted bilingual literacy support designed to reinforce grade-level standards and promote biliteracy through instruction in both English and their partner language. Student grouping is intentional and based on proficiency data from the NYSITELL, NYSESLAT, and the most recent EDUR, EDAT, and ATS-generated RFSF reports. Entering and Emerging ELLs will be grouped separately to ensure access to highly scaffolded instruction, while Transitioning and Commanding ELLs, as well as Newcomers with strong native-language literacy, will be grouped according to similar proficiency levels and reading benchmarks. Dual Language students will be grouped by grade level and bilingual proficiency within the Dual Language Academy. The After-School Program will run Monday through Thursday from 2:30 PM to 4:00 PM and will provide in-person small-group instruction aligned with the Science of Reading. This program will serve ELLs across all proficiency levels, students performing at Level 1 or 2 in ELA, and Dual Language students in a parallel academy. Certified classroom teachers, ENL-certified and bilingual teachers when available, and paraprofessionals will staff the program. Academic Recovery funds and Title III will support the staffing and materials. A Saturday Academy, offered tentatively from January through May, will operate from 9:00 AM to 12:00 PM and will focus on literacy enrichment and remediation for students who continue to exhibit notable challenges in language development and reading benchmarks. Saturday instruction will be delivered by ENL and ELA teachers and supported through Academic Recovery funding. Instruction throughout the programs will be rigorous, intentional, and personalized, emphasizing depth of understanding over pacing to ensure mastery of essential language and literacy skills. Lessons will be delivered in English with home-language supports to increase accessibility and comprehension. Culturally responsive and trauma-informed practices will be embedded throughout the program to support academic, linguistic, and social- emotional development. Approximately eighteen certified teachers will support the program, including ENL and Bilingual-certified teachers DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 234 of 283 who will provide additional ENL minutes for eligible students. Instructional materials will include Rigby Guided Reading kits and multisensory phonics programs such as Orton-Gillingham and Fundations, which support phonological awareness, phonics, vocabulary, fluency, comprehension, and writing. Digital tools—including Amira, iReady, the Wilson online platform, and Epic Books—will supplement instruction. Title III funds will be used to purchase the Measure Up learning program to further support ELLs. Students will participate in daily activities such as read-alouds, guided reading, sight-word review, multisensory phonics instruction, vocabulary front-loading, literacy games, close reading, social-emotional learning routines, and math and literacy bridging tasks that strengthen connections to core classroom instruction. Home-language scaffolds, including audio texts, first-language parallel texts through platforms such as SORA, visuals, music, artwork, and technology, will be used to expand access and deepen comprehension. Classes will include approximately fifteen to twenty students, with two classes per grade in kindergarten through third grade and one class per grade in fourth and fifth grade. Each class will be taught by a certified teacher. The school will maintain accurate attendance records, provide family communication in preferred languages, and monitor student progress using classroom assessments, ELL data reports, and administrative oversight. This systematic approach ensures that instruction remains responsive, data-driven, and aligned to the goal of accelerating student learning and supporting the linguistic and academic success of all multilingual learners at P.S. 7. Rationale for Title III ELL Funded Programs Describe the rationale for each Title III ELL funded program. For each program, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for program, and Data sources used to justify the need for the program Rationale: P.S. 7 recognizes the impact recent disruptions in education have had on all students' academic progress and social-emotional well-being, including those with Limited English Proficiency. In order to address the opportunity gap exacerbated during this period, P.S. 7 will implement an afternoon program aimed at addressing unfinished learning, learning loss, and students' social-emotional needs. The rationale for the program designed for Entering or Emerging ELL students is grounded in both quantitative and qualitative evidence. Analysis of the ELL Data Update Report (EDUR, 2025) indicates that 162 students, representing 18 percent of the school’s total population, scored at the Entering or Emerging levels on the 2025 NYSESLAT. Within this subgroup, fewer than 30 percent met expected growth targets in reading and writing, confirming the need for intensive intervention. Classroom-based formative assessments, including running records, demonstrate that 72 percent of these students read below grade level, while iReady and Acadience literacy diagnostics show that vocabulary acquisition lags an average of 1.5 grade levels behind. Teacher observations reinforce these findings, documenting consistent difficulties in producing simple and compound sentences in both oral and written forms. Collectively, these data sources demonstrate the urgent need for structured programming that accelerates English language development for Entering and Emerging learners. For Transitioning to Commanding ELL students, as well as recent arrivals who are not yet classified as ELLs, the rationale is equally supported by quantitative and qualitative data. A total of 351 students, or 39 percent of the school’s population, fall into this category. Although these students have achieved higher levels of English proficiency, analysis of NYSESLAT growth charts shows that only 54 percent made expected annual progress, DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 235 of 283 compared to 68 percent of their English-proficient peers. MAP Growth assessments reveal that 47 percent of this subgroup remain below grade-level benchmarks in reading comprehension, while Fountas & Pinnell benchmarks indicate that 41 percent demonstrate limited ability to sustain comprehension of complex texts. Writing rubric analyses highlight ongoing weaknesses in academic vocabulary use and complex sentence construction. Teacher performance data and classroom assessments further support the need for targeted programming, designed to ensure that these students sustain progress and meet grade-level expectations. For the 104 students who have reached Commanding proficiency, the rationale for continued support comes from data showing that language development remains incomplete even at this advanced level. While these students have exited formal ELL status, benchmark assessments reveal gaps in higher-order skills: iReady comprehension results show that 38 percent scored below proficiency in critical analysis tasks, and guided reading records confirm that 29 percent require ongoing support to interpret figurative language and abstract concepts in text. Teacher observations and writing portfolio reviews further highlight that nearly one-third of Commanding-level students struggle to apply academic discourse effectively in extended written assignments. Without sustained intervention, these students are at risk of plateauing academically, leaving them underprepared for advanced coursework in later grades. By grounding its Title III ELL funded programs in multiple data sources—including the 2025 NYSESLAT, EDUR reports, MAP Growth, iReady, Acadience, Fountas & Pinnell benchmarks, classroom assessments, and teacher observations—P.S. 7 ensures that programming is directly aligned to student need. This data-driven approach allows the school to deliver equitable, targeted supports that promote language acquisition, strengthen academic achievement, and meet the social-emotional needs of ELL students across all levels of proficiency. Students and Grade Levels to be Served P.S. 7’s Title III program will serve a total of 617 English Language Learners in Grades K–5, representing students across the full continuum of language proficiency levels, from newcomers to long-term English proficient students. Programming is designed to address the diverse needs of these learners by tailoring supports to their language development stage and academic goals. The first component of the program will focus on newcomer students, primarily at the Entering and Emerging levels of English proficiency. A total of 162 students in Grades K–5, representing 26 percent of the total ELL population, fall into this subgroup. These students typically demonstrate very limited English proficiency and require intensive foundational support in order to develop vocabulary, build reading accuracy and fluency, and produce simple and compound sentences both orally and in writing. The second component of the program will serve 351 students in Grades K–5, or 57 percent of the total ELL population, who are identified as developing ELLs or recent arrivals who are not officially classified as ELLs but still require targeted language and literacy support. These students are at the Transitioning or Expanding levels of proficiency and are progressing toward fluency and academic language proficiency in English. The program will DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 236 of 283 focus on strengthening their reading accuracy, fluency, comprehension, and their ability to construct and communicate using more complex sentences across academic contexts. Finally, the support program for long-term ELLs (LTEs) will address the needs of 104 students in Grades K–5, representing 17 percent of the total ELL population, who have achieved Commanding proficiency on the NYSESLAT but continue to demonstrate gaps in higher-order comprehension and application of academic language. These students will participate in small group instruction and guided reading practices designed to deepen their understanding of complex texts and improve their ability to apply advanced language skills in academic tasks. Through this comprehensive approach, P.S. 7’s Title III program ensures that all 617 ELLs in Grades K–5—spanning newcomers, developing learners, and long-term ELLs—receive targeted instruction aligned with their language proficiency levels, thereby supporting equitable access to academic achievement and closing opportunity gaps. Schedule and Duration For each Title III ELL funded program, include: Start and end dates/months, Total number of sessions, Days of the week the program will be offered, and Time and duration of sessions. Schedule and Duration All components of the Title III program at P.S. 7 will run from Monday, October 6, 2025, through Friday, June 5, 2026. Sessions will take place Monday through Thursday from 2:30 p.m. to 4:00 p.m., providing one and a half hours of extended learning time on each instructional day. Over the course of the year, this schedule will result in approximately 32 instructional weeks. With four sessions each week, students will have access to a total of about 128 sessions during the program cycle. This equates to roughly 192 hours of additional instructional support for each group of students. The program for Entering or Emerging ELL students will operate on this schedule, offering consistent small-group and targeted support across the school year. The program designed for Transitioning–Commanding level ELL students and recent arrivals will also run on the same four-day weekly cycle, with a total of approximately 128 sessions. Finally, the support program for Commanding-level ELL students will follow the identical schedule and duration, ensuring equity in access across all proficiency groups. By structuring all three programs to run concurrently from October 6, 2025, through June 5, 2026, and by maintaining a steady schedule of Monday through Thursday afternoon sessions, P.S. 7 ensures that ELL students at all levels receive sustained and predictable support. The estimated 128 sessions, or 192 instructional hours, for each program provide ample time for targeted instruction, skill-building, and progress monitoring throughout the school year. At P.S. 7, each Title III ELL-funded program is supported by a dedicated team of certified teachers, ensuring that immigrant students receive the specialized instruction they need. The staff is carefully selected to address the unique needs of immigrant students at different stages of language acquisition. For the program serving immigrant students classified as Entering or Emerging ELLs, the school will assign certified teachers who specialize in ESOL (English to Speakers of Other Languages) instruction. These educators will provide foundational support in English language development. Additionally, content area teachers will be involved as necessary to ensure that immigrant students receive integrated support in both language development and academic subjects, helping them succeed across the curriculum. The program designed for immigrant students at the Transitioning to Commanding level, as well as recent arrivals who are not classified as ELLs but still require additional support, will also be staffed with a team of certified teachers. These include ESOL-certified educators who specialize in advancing reading fluency, comprehension, and language proficiency. To further meet the needs of these students, content area teachers will work alongside the ESOL team to provide targeted instruction that aligns with both academic and language goals, ensuring immigrant students make progress in all areas of their education. For the support program targeting immigrant students at the Commanding level, small group instruction will be the focus. Certified teachers proficient in both ESOL methodologies and content area subjects will work with students to strengthen their comprehension skills and academic performance. These educators will employ specialized teaching strategies to ensure that immigrant students continue to build on their academic strengths while deepening their understanding of complex concepts. Additionally, the Dual Language After-School Program will be staffed by two certified bilingual teachers—one fluent in Chinese and the other in Spanish. These teachers will provide instruction in both languages, fostering bilingual development and supporting academic achievement in both English and the students' home language. This bilingual approach offers immigrant students the opportunity to strengthen their language skills while receiving comprehensive support in their academic subjects. Each program's staffing structure reflects P.S. 7’s commitment to providing high-quality, specialized instruction for immigrant students. By leveraging certified teachers’ expertise in ESOL, bilingual education, and content areas, the school ensures that immigrant students receive the targeted support necessary for their academic and linguistic success. Staffing details, including the exact number of teachers and their specific certifications, will be finalized based on program enrollment and needs as the school year progresses. Rationale for Professional Development Describe the rationale for the professional development to be provided. Rationale for Professional Development at P.S. 7: Data-Driven Approach At P.S. 7, professional development initiatives under the Title III ELL program are intentionally aligned with the specific needs of our English Language Learners, based on detailed data analysis. This ensures that instructional strategies are responsive and effective in promoting both linguistic and academic growth for students across all proficiency levels. Student Demographics and NYSESLAT 2025 Results The school serves a total of 617 students identified as ELLs and Former ELLs. Of these, 162 students are classified as Entering or Emerging ELLs, representing foundational language learners who require intensive support in English vocabulary, reading accuracy, fluency, and sentence construction. Another 351 students are identified as Transitioning to Commanding level ELLs or recent arrivals, who are progressing toward academic language proficiency and require support in reading comprehension, academic vocabulary, and complex writing skills. The remaining 104 students have achieved Commanding-level proficiency, indicating successful transition to Former ELL status, yet they continue to need targeted instruction in content-area literacy, particularly in science and social studies. The 2025 NYSESLAT results highlight these proficiency levels and inform professional development needs. Data analysis indicates that while students at the Commanding level demonstrate conversational English proficiency, approximately 80 percent of these students still require support to master academic language and content-area literacy. Among the 351 Transitioning-level students, formative assessments reveal that roughly 65 percent are still developing fluency and comprehension skills, and among the 162 Entering/Emerging students, nearly 90 percent require targeted support to bridge learning gaps in foundational literacy skills. Identified Trends and Professional Development Needs The data reveal three critical areas for professional development. First, Entering and Emerging ELL students require intensive literacy instruction, including multi-sensory phonics, guided reading, and vocabulary-building strategies. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 243 of 283 Second, Transitioning and Commanding-level students need support to strengthen comprehension, academic writing, and content-area language, ensuring that they can access grade-level curriculum effectively. Third, social- emotional learning emerged as a key priority, with teacher feedback indicating that approximately 40 percent of ELL and Former ELL students need strategies to manage emotional well-being and engagement, particularly following the disruptions caused by remote learning. Additionally, culturally responsive teaching practices are essential to ensure all 617 students feel included and represented in classroom instruction. Professional Development Plan In response to these identified needs, the Title III professional development plan for 2025–2026 is designed to equip 12 certified teachers with strategies that directly address student needs across all proficiency levels. Teachers working with Entering/Emerging students will receive PD focused on intensive literacy interventions, including Fundations and Orton-Gillingham phonics instruction, guided reading, and strategies to support vocabulary acquisition for the 162 students in this group. Professional development for teachers supporting the 351 Transitioning/Commanding students emphasizes strategies to scaffold academic language, strengthen reading comprehension, and support writing in complex sentences. For the 104 Commanding-level students, PD will focus on maintaining and extending advanced literacy skills, particularly in content-rich areas such as social studies and science. Across all programs, teachers will also receive training in social-emotional learning and culturally responsive teaching practices to support engagement, inclusion, and equity for the entire student population. Monitoring and Impact The effectiveness of professional development will be monitored through ongoing assessment of student outcomes, including NYSESLAT scores, classroom formative assessments, and teacher observations. Progress will be tracked for all 617 ELL and Former ELL students, with specific attention to the 162 Entering/Emerging students, the 351 Transitioning/Commanding students, and the 104 Commanding-level students. These data will guide adjustments to instructional strategies and professional development throughout the year, ensuring that all PD activities are directly tied to measurable improvements in student literacy, language proficiency, and academic achievement. By aligning professional development with detailed numerical data for each Title III program, P.S. 7 ensures that teachers are fully prepared to meet the diverse academic and social-emotional needs of ELL students. This data- driven approach supports continued growth for current ELLs, sustained achievement for Former ELLs, and equitable learning outcomes for all students. At P.S. 7, the professional development plan is designed to provide ongoing, sustained support for teachers to meet the specific needs of English Language Learners (ELLs) and to promote high academic achievement across all core subjects. Each professional development activity is intentionally aligned with the Title III program structure, ensuring that instruction in the classroom and afterschool programs consistently reinforces language development, literacy skills, and academic growth in both English and students’ home languages. Teachers will engage in continuous professional learning focused on analyzing Acadience and MAP Growth assessment data to inform instruction. These sessions are designed to support 162 Entering and Emerging ELL students, 351 Transitioning to Commanding level ELL students, and 104 Commanding-level students. During regular collaborative data meetings, teachers examine assessment results using the ladder of inference to identify individual student needs and monitor progress over time. This ongoing practice ensures that instructional strategies are tailored to accelerate growth in literacy and language development, with targeted interventions for students at every proficiency level. Professional development will also provide sustained guidance on creating differentiated word work stations for Kindergarten and first-grade students. Using ongoing progress monitoring data from Fundations and Heggerty, teachers will adjust these stations throughout the year to support letter and sound recognition, phonemic awareness, and decoding skills. This PD activity directly benefits the 52 Entering and Emerging students in Kindergarten and 48 in first grade, allowing instruction to meet each student’s current language proficiency level and ensuring foundational literacy growth for long-term reading success. In addition, teachers will participate in extended professional learning focused on shared reading, shared writing, and guided reading strategies. These sessions involve video modeling, peer observation, and collaborative lesson planning that continues throughout the year. This ongoing PD supports the 162 Entering/Emerging students, 351 Transitioning-level students, and 104 Commanding-level students by providing structured, language-rich lessons that improve comprehension, fluency, and written expression, particularly critical for ELLs who require scaffolded literacy experiences. Scaffolding reading and writing instruction for ELLs is another focus of sustained professional development. Teachers will work throughout the year to build background knowledge, develop small-group scaffolding strategies, and differentiate instruction so that students can access grade-level content while advancing English proficiency. This ongoing training supports all 617 ELL and Former ELL students, helping them gradually gain independence in complex academic tasks and fully participate in classroom discussions. For the afterschool program, teachers will receive continuous professional learning aligned with daytime instruction. This includes training on program goals, instructional strategies, and effective use of materials, with follow-up sessions to monitor implementation and student progress. These activities directly support the 162 Entering/Emerging students, 351 Transitioning-level students, and 104 Commanding-level students enrolled in Title III-funded programs, ensuring consistency between day and afterschool learning and reinforcing language development, literacy skills, and academic growth. Through these ongoing, interconnected professional development activities, teachers at P.S. 7 are equipped to DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. At P.S. 7, family engagement activities under the Title III ELL program are designed to build a strong partnership between school and home, empowering parents of ELL students to actively support their children’s learning. These activities are aligned with Title III requirements, ensuring that both staff and OTPS costs are tracked and maintained within federal limits, with teacher per-session costs capped at 10 percent of the total Title III allocation and vendor/material costs capped at 20 percent. For the Title III LEP Extended Day Program, parents of ELL students, particularly immigrant families, will participate in seven structured workshops throughout the 2025–2026 school year. These workshops will be facilitated by certified teachers and assistant principals, including Orit Sperber and Tanya Ritter, and supported by bilingual paraprofessionals fluent in Chinese, Spanish, and Bengali. Each session is designed to provide families with actionable strategies to support language development, literacy, and academic growth at home. Workshops are scheduled on the first Wednesday of every month from 1:30 PM to 2:20 PM. The workshop topics are carefully selected to meet the specific needs of ELL families. Sessions will cover using remote technology and online learning platforms such as Google Classroom and Zoom, strategies for supporting children’s learning at home, methods to assist struggling readers through sight word practice, interactive read-aloud activities, hands-on literacy games, understanding what it means to be an English Language Learner, and an orientation for new students entering kindergarten. These workshops also provide guidance on the Middle School Application Process for families of older students. Outreach and communication about these workshops are conducted in multiple formats to ensure accessibility for all families. Notices are sent through translated letters and flyers, posted on the school website, Instagram, Class Dojo, and Google Classroom, and shared via phone announcements by the parent coordinator. Teachers reinforce participation by discussing upcoming workshops during parent meetings and communicating through Class Dojo. Title III funds will be used to cover teacher time and OTPS costs for materials and any vendor-provided resources. Teacher compensation for facilitating each workshop is calculated as part of the 10 percent maximum allocation for staff time under Title III, with seven sessions totaling approximately 14 teacher hours. OTPS expenditures for materials such as take-home literacy games, printed resources, and interactive activity supplies are estimated to remain under 20 percent of the total Title III allocation. Simeone 257 of 283 Title III Immigrant Outline Title III Immigrant Outline Title III Application for Immigrant Funding REQUIREMENTS Title III, Part A - Immigrant Funds can be used for activities that provide enhanced instructional opportunities for immigrant students, which include: Identification and acquisition of curricular materials, educational software and technologies to be used in the program. Family literacy, parent outreach and training activities designed to assist parents in becoming active participants in the education of their children and understand their rights and the benefits of multilingualism. Support for personnel, including teacher aides, parent coordinators, and school counselors who have been specifically trained, or are being trained, to provide services to immigrant students and families. Provision of tutorials, mentoring, and academic or career counseling for immigrant students, as well as engagement in pre-college and work-based learning experiences. Basic instructional services directly attributable to the aforementioned school districts, including costs for additional classroom supplies, transportation costs, or other costs directly attributable to such services. Other instructional services that are designed to assist immigrant students to achieve in elementary schools and secondary schools in the United States, such as programs of introduction to the educational system and civics education. School activities, coordinated with community-based organizations, institutions of higher education, private sector entities, or other entities with expertise in working with immigrants, to assist families of immigrant students by offering comprehensive community services. Funding follows similar guidelines for Title III funds established by the Office of Multilingual Learners (OML) and can only be used to provide supplementary services to immigrant students, who may or may not be English Language Learners (ELLs). Please note: Immigrant students are not necessarily English Language Learners (ELLs), and ELLs may or may not be immigrants. For the purpose of this funding, immigrant students are defined as individuals who were not born in any U.S. state (this includes the District of Columbia and the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico); and have not been attending one or more schools in any one or more states for more than three full, academic years (the months need not be consecutive). DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 258 of 283 Priority areas for the use of Title III, Part A - Immigrant Funds are as follows: Extended day programming (e.g., before school, after school, Saturday program) to provide targeted, small-group instruction, credit recovery support, and postsecondary preparation. Implementing strong student supports to increase graduation rates. Community-based and school-based programming (e.g., before school, after school, Saturday program) to strengthen partnerships between immigrant families and schools. Developing new and/or enhancing existing programs for newcomers (meeting criteria defined above). Supplementing the opening or growth of Transitional Bilingual Education programs and/or Dual Language Bilingual Education programs serving immigrant students. Professional learning on meeting the needs of immigrant students, creating a welcoming and inclusive school environment, and effective instructional practices. For more information on Title III requirements, refer to NYSED’s guidance on allowable and unallowable expenditures for Title III. For specific allocation amounts per school, see the School Allocation Memo or contact your ML/ELL Director or ELL Compliance and Performance Specialist. The Title III Afterschool Program will operate during the 2025–2026 school year from October 6, 2025 through April 24, 2026 and is designed to provide additional academic support to immigrant students as defined under Title III— students not born in the United States who have attended U.S. schools for three years or fewer. Based on current enrollment records, PS 7Q serves 167 immigrant students within its English Language Learner (ELL) population. These students are part of a broader ELL population of 373 students, highlighting that while many immigrant students are also identified as ELLs, immigrant status is determined by country of birth and length of time in U.S. schools, not by English language proficiency. The program takes place from 2:30 PM to 4:00 PM, Monday through Thursday, and provides a structured and supportive learning environment focused on literacy development. Instruction emphasizes evidence-based literacy DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 261 of 283 practices, including Fundations Double-Dose activities, Rigby Guided Reading, and targeted small-group instruction. These approaches address both English language acquisition and grade-level academic expectations. Fundations Double-Dose strengthens phonics and foundational reading skills, while Rigby Guided Reading supports reading comprehension and oral language development. Small-group instruction allows teachers to tailor lessons to students’ specific instructional needs. Students are grouped based on grade level and instructional language needs, with teachers further organizing students into flexible small groups according to targeted skill areas. Afterschool classes typically consist of 20–25 students, divided into no more than five small groups of approximately five students each. This structure allows for individualized attention while promoting peer collaboration among students with similar learning needs. Instruction is delivered through a combination of explicit instruction and, when appropriate, team teaching. Explicit instruction ensures clarity and consistency in literacy skill development, while team teaching enables educators to better support students with varied language and academic needs. Instructional practices are adjusted as needed based on ongoing assessment data and student progress. The program is closely supervised by school administration, which oversees implementation and ensures alignment with instructional best practices. Administration also provides professional development for teachers focused on guided reading strategies and the effective use of student data to inform small-group instruction. Costs associated with supervision and professional development are supported through general school funding and are not charged to Title III Immigrant funds. Through structured literacy instruction, small-group learning, and responsive teaching practices, the Title III Afterschool Program supports the academic and linguistic growth of immigrant students within the ELL population. By strengthening literacy skills while reinforcing grade-level content, the program helps ensure that eligible immigrant students make meaningful academic progress during the 2025–2026 school year. Rationale for Title III Immigrant Funded Programs Describe the rationale for each Title III Immigrant funded program: For each program, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for program, and Data sources used to justify the need for the program P.S. 7 recognizes the profound impact recent disruptions in education have had on all students, particularly English Language Learners (ELLs). These disruptions have created significant learning gaps, affecting academic progress and social-emotional well-being. For students still developing English language skills, the need for targeted support has never been greater. In response, P.S. 7 has launched an afternoon program designed to address unfinished learning, support social-emotional needs, and help students progress academically. The need for this program is supported by both qualitative and quantitative data, including ongoing assessments, language proficiency tests, teacher observations, and classroom performance indicators. These data identify specific areas where students require targeted intervention and form the foundation for the program’s design. A total of 100% of immigrant students at P.S. 7—those not born in the United States who have been enrolled in U.S. schools for three years or fewer—are served across grades K–5, with 15% in Kindergarten, 17% in Grade 1, 21% in Grade 2, 20% in Grade 3, 15% in Grade 4, and 12% in Grade 5. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 262 of 283 Among the general ELL population, 41% are at the Entering or Emerging level, indicating they are in the early stages of English language acquisition. These students require foundational instruction in vocabulary development, reading fluency, and constructing simple and compound sentences. 60% of ELLs are at the Transitioning and Commanding levels, needing targeted support to strengthen reading comprehension, academic vocabulary, and the ability to write and speak using more complex sentence structures. 27% of ELLs are at the Commanding level, where the focus is on extending comprehension, critical thinking, and engagement with complex academic texts. By continuously collecting and analyzing data from the ELL Data Updated Report (EDUR), NYSESLAT results, classroom assessments, and teacher observations, P.S. 7 ensures that instructional decisions are responsive to students’ evolving needs. Targeted small-group instruction, guided reading, and focused language development strategies provide all students—both immigrant and general ELL—with the support necessary to close achievement gaps, meet grade-level expectations, and develop the skills needed for long-term academic success. Simeone 265 of 283 For each Title III Immigrant funded program, include language of instruction and how home language support is provided: At P.S. 7, each Title III ELL-funded program is supported by a dedicated team of certified teachers who are specifically trained to meet the needs of immigrant students. These teachers are carefully selected to address the diverse linguistic and academic needs of immigrant students at various stages of language acquisition. For the program serving immigrant students classified as Entering or Emerging ELLs, the school will assign certified teachers who specialize in ESOL (English to Speakers of Other Languages) instruction. These educators will provide essential support in building the foundational English skills needed for academic success. In addition, content area teachers will collaborate with the ESOL team to ensure that immigrant students receive integrated support across both language development and core subjects, helping them thrive academically in all areas. The program designed for immigrant students at the Transitioning to Commanding level, as well as recent immigrant arrivals who are not yet classified as ELLs but still require additional support, will also be staffed with a team of certified teachers. These teachers include ESOL-certified educators who specialize in enhancing reading fluency, comprehension, and overall language proficiency. Content area teachers will work alongside the ESOL instructors to provide targeted instruction that addresses both language development and academic growth, ensuring that immigrant students continue to make progress in all areas of their education. For the support program targeting immigrant students at the Commanding level, the focus will be on small group instruction led by certified teachers proficient in both ESOL methods and content area subjects. These educators will work with immigrant students to deepen their comprehension skills and further strengthen their academic performance. Specialized instructional strategies will be employed to ensure that immigrant students continue to build on their existing strengths while gaining a deeper understanding of more complex academic content. Additionally, P.S. 7's Dual Language After-School Program will be staffed by two certified bilingual teachers, one fluent in Chinese and the other in Spanish. These teachers will provide instruction in both languages, promoting bilingual development and supporting academic achievement in both English and students' home languages. This bilingual approach allows immigrant students to further develop their language skills while receiving support in academic subjects, providing a well-rounded educational experience. The staffing structure for each program reflects P.S. 7’s commitment to providing high-quality, specialized instruction that meets the unique needs of immigrant students. By utilizing certified teachers with expertise in ESOL, bilingual education, and content-area instruction, the school ensures that immigrant students receive the focused support necessary to succeed both linguistically and academically. Staffing details, including the number of teachers and their certifications, will be finalized based on program enrollment and needs as the school year progresses.
