Simeone 270 of 283 Rationale for Professional Development Describe the rationale for the professional development to be provided. For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., patterns and trends identified) that support the need for the professional development, and Data sources (e.g., school survey teacher responses, in-house teacher surveys, teacher observation data) used to justify the professional development. For each activity include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for the professional development Data sources used to justify the professional development At P.S. 7, the professional development (PD) plan under the Title III ELL program is intentionally designed to address documented instructional needs identified through both quantitative student data and qualitative teacher feedback. Analysis of language proficiency data, academic performance trends, and teacher observations indicates that educators require additional training to effectively support immigrant students at varying stages of English language development, particularly in literacy, academic language, and social-emotional learning. Current enrollment data show that 35.09% of immigrant students are classified as English Language Learners (ELLs), 9.32% are Former ELLs, and 55.58% are Non-ELLs. While ELL students are actively developing English proficiency, data indicate that Former ELLs—despite having exited ELL status—continue to experience challenges with advanced academic language, particularly in content areas such as science and social studies. NYSESLAT results further show that 106 immigrant students have reached the Commanding level, demonstrating strong conversational English skills but continued need for support with complex vocabulary, syntax, and academic discourse. These trends highlight a clear instructional need for teachers to strengthen strategies for supporting academic language development beyond basic proficiency. Qualitative data from teacher feedback and classroom observations reinforce these findings. Teachers report difficulty supporting students who appear fluent in English but struggle with reading comprehension, content- specific vocabulary, and extended writing tasks. As a result, educators have identified a need for professional development focused on advanced literacy strategies, scaffolding academic language, and supporting language development across content areas. In response to these identified needs, the PD plan prioritizes targeted training aligned directly to instructional gaps. First, teachers will receive training focused on addressing unfinished learning and literacy gaps, with an emphasis on strategies that support both ELLs and Former ELLs. This training responds directly to assessment data indicating persistent gaps in reading comprehension, writing, and academic vocabulary among immigrant students, particularly those transitioning out of ELL status. Second, teacher feedback has highlighted the need for greater capacity in supporting students’ social-emotional learning (SEL). Many immigrant students continue to experience challenges related to confidence, engagement, and classroom participation, particularly following disruptions to learning. As a result, the PD plan includes SEL-focused training to help teachers create emotionally supportive learning environments that promote student engagement and academic risk-taking. Finally, the PD plan includes a strong focus on culturally responsive teaching (CRT). Teachers have identified the need for strategies that affirm students’ linguistic and cultural identities while strengthening instructional relevance. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 271 of 283 Training in culturally responsive practices will support teachers in creating inclusive classrooms and delivering instruction that is responsive to the diverse backgrounds of immigrant students. The effectiveness of the PD plan will be monitored through ongoing analysis of formative assessments such as Acadience and MAP Growth, as well as NYSESLAT results and classroom-based data. These measures will allow the school to assess whether professional learning is translating into improved instructional practices and stronger student outcomes. Teachers will use these data to adjust instruction and refine strategies, ensuring continuous alignment between professional development, instructional practice, and student need. Through this data-driven and needs-aligned professional development plan, P.S. 7 is building teacher capacity to address both the academic and social-emotional needs of immigrant students. By directly connecting student performance trends and teacher-identified needs to targeted PD topics, the school ensures that professional learning is purposeful, responsive, and effective in supporting long-term student success. Teachers to Receive Professional Development Include information on the teachers to receive professional development. Please specify how all teachers working in the Title III Immigrant program will receive ongoing professional development related to the Title III Immigrant program. At P.S. 7, professional development under the Title III ELL program is provided to teachers who directly serve English Language Learners and immigrant students. This includes classroom teachers in grades K–5 with ELLs in their classes, Title III afterschool teachers, ENL-certified teachers, literacy intervention teachers, and instructional staff who provide small-group or push-in language support. These educators participate in ongoing professional development designed to strengthen instructional practices and improve outcomes for immigrant students and English Language Learners. Participating teachers engage in training focused on several critical instructional areas aligned to student needs. First, teachers receive professional development in differentiated instruction, with an emphasis on adapting lessons to meet the varied language proficiency levels of immigrant students. Training supports teachers in applying these strategies in in-person instructional settings, ensuring that students receive appropriate scaffolds and supports regardless of their level of English proficiency. Professional development also emphasizes culturally responsive teaching. Teachers working directly with immigrant and ELL students are trained to integrate students’ cultural and linguistic backgrounds into instruction, creating inclusive classrooms where students feel valued and supported. This training helps teachers build stronger relationships with students and implement equitable instructional practices that promote engagement and academic success. Supporting the social-emotional well-being of immigrant students is another key focus of the professional development. Classroom teachers, ENL teachers, and intervention staff receive training in strategies such as mindfulness practices and trauma-informed approaches that help create emotionally supportive learning environments. These strategies are particularly important in supporting students who have experienced educational disruptions or transitions. In addition, teachers who provide ELL and immigrant student instruction receive targeted training in language acquisition strategies. This professional development focuses on modeling effective oral language practices, explicit vocabulary instruction, and strategies that promote student participation and academic discourse. Teachers apply these strategies during literacy instruction and content-area lessons to support English language development alongside academic learning. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Analyzing Acadience and MAP Growth Data to Inform Instruction Teachers will use the ladder of inference to analyze assessment data and identify specific student needs, including literacy and language gaps common among immigrant students and SIFE learners. By interpreting data through a lens of cultural and educational context, teachers can design instruction that responds to both academic and transitional needs. How this supports student success: DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Simeone 273 of 283 Enables personalized instruction that accounts for disrupted educational backgrounds. Helps identify gaps in foundational knowledge and language proficiency. Supports targeted interventions that accelerate literacy and language development for all learners, particularly newcomers adjusting to U.S. academic expectations. 2. Creating Differentiated Word Work Stations in Kindergarten and First Grade Using data from the Fundations and Heggerty programs, teachers will develop hands-on, differentiated word work stations to support early literacy development. Instruction will include strategies to support students with interrupted education or limited English exposure, emphasizing phonemic awareness, decoding, and language acquisition. How this supports student success: Builds foundational literacy skills at an individualized pace, crucial for SIFE students who may have gaps in prior schooling. Provides multi-sensory, culturally responsive learning opportunities for diverse learners. Helps students gain confidence and early mastery of essential literacy skills, setting the stage for long-term academic success. 3. Shared Reading, Shared Writing, and Guided Reading Teachers will explore best practices for shared reading, shared writing, and guided reading lessons, with additional emphasis on scaffolding content for newcomers and immigrant students. PD will include modeling strategies to activate prior knowledge, build schema for U.S. academic norms, and support comprehension for students unfamiliar with typical classroom expectations. How this supports student success: Strengthens language-rich interactions that are essential for ELLs. Supports the gradual integration of students into U.S. academic systems. Enhances engagement and confidence in literacy tasks for students navigating cultural and linguistic transitions. 4. Scaffolding Reading and Writing to Meet the Needs of ELLs and SIFE Students This session will focus on scaffolding strategies for students with limited or interrupted formal education. Teachers will learn to provide tiered supports, develop background knowledge, and create structured pathways to grade-level literacy and content mastery. Trauma-informed practices will also be integrated to address the emotional and psychological needs of newcomer students. How this supports student success: Ensures access to rigorous content while supporting language development. Addresses gaps in prior schooling and facilitates smoother transitions into the U.S. academic system. Engaging Families New to the U.S. Education System Teachers will receive PD on effective strategies for partnering with families who are new to the U.S. school system. This includes culturally responsive communication, understanding parental expectations shaped by different educational systems, and involving families in literacy and language development at home. How this supports student success: Strengthens the home-school connection, which is critical for student achievement. Provides families with the knowledge and tools to support their children’s learning. Encourages culturally inclusive practices that validate students’ backgrounds and foster community engagement. 6. Afterschool Program Professional Development Teachers in the afterschool program will receive training aligned with daytime instructional practices, emphasizing continuity for immigrant and ELL students. Sessions will address differentiated support for students with interrupted education and provide strategies to reinforce literacy and language acquisition in a supportive setting. How this supports student success: Ensures consistent, high-quality academic support across learning environments. Reinforces targeted literacy and language interventions. Provides additional scaffolding for students adjusting to the U.S. academic and cultural context. At P.S. 7, our family engagement activities within the Title III ELL program are designed to build a strong partnership between the school and immigrant families. Supported by Title III funding, these activities aim to empower immigrant parents through informative workshops and support services tailored to their needs, ensuring they can actively participate in their children’s educational journey. The Title III LEP Extended Day Program includes a series of workshops for immigrant parents, with the goal of helping them better understand how to support their children, particularly those in the ELL program, as they navigate their learning experiences. These workshops are led by experienced educators, including Orit Sperber and Tanya Ritter, who specialize in working with immigrant families. The workshops are held monthly and are open to immigrant families of ELL students. There are a total of seven sessions, each focusing on a different aspect of supporting children’s education and language development. The workshops take place on the first Wednesday of each month from 1:30 PM to 2:20 PM. The topics covered in these workshops include: 1. Utilizing Remote Technology and Online Platforms (such as Google Classroom and Zoom) 2. Strategies to Support Your Child’s Learning at Home 3. How to Help Your Struggling Reader at Home by Practicing Sight Words 4. The Power of Read Alouds: An Interactive Experience 5. Make and Take Literacy Games 6. What Does it Mean to Be an English Language Learner? 7. New Student Orientation and the Kindergarten School Experience These sessions are specifically designed for immigrant parents, with a focus on helping them understand how to navigate the school system, support their children's academic progress, and get familiar with the ELL program offerings. The workshops also provide information about key milestones in a child’s educational journey, such as the Middle School Application Process. The workshops are facilitated by teachers, assistant principals, and bilingual paraprofessionals fluent in languages such as Chinese, Spanish, and Bengali, ensuring that all immigrant parents can fully participate. To promote these sessions, information is shared through translated letters, flyers, and posts on the school website, Instagram, Class Dojo, and Google Classroom. Teachers also keep parents informed through individual meetings, and the parent coordinator uses a phone announcement system to ensure outreach to all families. DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. Rationale for Family Engagement Activities Describe the rationale for the family engagement activities to be provided: For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., reasons for topics, time activities will be offered), and Data sources (e.g., parent survey responses, in-house parent surveys, PTA/PA request) used to justify the need for activities. At P.S. 7, our rationale for family engagement activities is grounded in a strong commitment to creating a supportive and inclusive school community, where immigrant families play an essential role in their children’s academic success. We recognize that when schools and families work together as partners, student achievement and well- being significantly improve. This belief is central to our approach, which is informed by both qualitative and quantitative data that highlights the need for meaningful opportunities for immigrant families to engage in their children's education. Through surveys conducted by our Parent Coordinator, Tanya Melito, we gather insights directly from immigrant families about their preferences, needs, and areas where they seek support. This feedback helps us tailor our family engagement activities, ensuring they address the specific concerns of immigrant parents. The surveys have highlighted several key areas of interest, including the use of technology at home, supporting English language learning, and strategies to enhance academic progress. These identified needs shape our workshop topics and the events we offer to parents. We draw on data sources like parent surveys and assessments of parent involvement in school activities to justify the need for these family engagement initiatives. Immigrant families have expressed a strong desire to participate more in their children’s education, and they value opportunities to understand classroom practices, assist with academic work, and navigate educational technology. By providing workshops on topics such as using digital tools at home, strategies for supporting English language learners, and ways to encourage reading and writing skills, we empower immigrant parents with the knowledge and skills needed to actively support their children's academic progress. Our commitment to inclusivity is evident in how we ensure that language barriers do not prevent immigrant families from fully participating in school activities. We provide translated notifications, interpreters, and language support during events to accommodate the diverse linguistic backgrounds of our families. This ensures that every parent, regardless of their primary language, has equal access to the information and resources available through our family DBN: 24Q007 School: P.S. 007 Louis F. These workshops are integral to our family engagement strategy, ensuring that parents are well-informed and equipped to support their children's academic and social-emotional growth effectively.
